 
Modelling Two Decades of Change and Future Developments 
in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) Ecosystem  
Using the Ecopath with Ecosim Toolbox

i 
ABSTRACT 
The Gulf of Nicoya is a highly productive estuary located on the Pacific coast of Costa Rica 
and accounts for the country’s primary fish production. It is increasingly impacted by its 
fisheries and climate variations, urbanization, tourism developments, the input of pollutants 
from urban centres, and agriculture activities in the catchment areas of large rivers entering the 
gulf.  Thus, our study intensively assesses the Gulf of Nicoya’s past, present, and future states 
and the main driver of ecosystem change.  The state of the ecosystem, food-web dynamics, 
detectable patterns in fishing efforts, and the external drivers’ influence on the system were 
analyzed. Also, the economic impacts of fishing effort regulations were simulated to test and 
propose possible management plans. A spatial model was also built to test the effect of a 
temporary fishing closure on the stock recovery of target species in the Gulf of Nicoya and to 
propose management alternatives. 
In the early 1990s, ecological and fishery data from the Gulf of Nicoya were holistically 
analyzed, and a trophic model was constructed using the Ecopath modelling approach. The 
results indicated that this tropical estuary, a hotspot for Costa Rican fisheries, was already 
overexploited by shrimp trawlers and longline fleets, and recent observations suggest further 
deterioration in this system. To evaluate the ecosystem and socio-economic changes in the Gulf 
of Nicoya over the last 20 years, the 1993 model was reconstructed with data from 2013 to 
compare both system states. Although the summary statistics of both states (i.e., 1993 and 2013) 
suggest that the system maintained its general functionality and even enabled the total harvest 
to increase by approximately 20%, a more detailed analysis of the levels of the functional 
groups led to the conclusion that the system was further degraded, causing a tremendous 
economic loss of approximately 50%. Like other coastal ecosystems, the Gulf of Nicoya is 
impacted by its fisheries, anthropogenic activities, and pollution from the large rivers entering 
the gulf. Thus, the gulf is particularly sensitive to short- and long-term changes in the climate, 
especially during extreme conditions in El Niño periods. For further analysis, we used a holistic 
approach to examine the impacts of fishing and environmental changes to identify the main 
drivers in the observed changes in the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem. While the model simulations 
indicate that fisheries’ exploitation rates mainly drive variations in the catchment, several 
species are also substantially affected by climate variations. This factor must be considered 
alongside implementing fishing regulations to form and develop appropriate management 
strategies. This dissertation tested the effectiveness of different management scenarios applied 
to the Gulf of Nicoya, including top-down and participatory fishing policies. Besides testing 
the current policy (which bans shrimp trawling), we used the automated fishing policy search 
tool of the EwE software to explore an alternative, optimized management scenario. The 
analysis indicates that the ban on trawling is important for the ecosystem’s recovery. However, 
additional measures might be needed, such as reducing fishing efforts by semi-industrial purse 
seine fleets and artisanal longline fleets. We present two possible alternative management 
scenarios with different balances of ecosystem conservation and economic losses. We 
complement our extensive analysis by adding a spatial component using the Ecospace tool, 
which can simulate species’ spatial distribution and fishing fleet effort over time. One obvious 
application is the assessment of the efficiency of existing and potential locations of MPAs to 
explore spatial management options and address various research questions, including the 
ii 
regional consequences of climate change. The approach was used to test the effectiveness of 
the existing annual seasonal fishing closure in the gulf (known as “Veda” in Spanish) and to 
tackle further questions. For example, is excluding all fleets from the gulf necessary during 
fishing closure? Would a seasonal closure covering the entire area of the Gulf of Nicoya be 
more efficient?  
This intensive assessment of the state of the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem leads to the conclusion 
that a drastic decrease in fishing effort, especially of semi-industrial fleets, is needed to maintain 
the ecosystem’s functionality, rebuild the biomass of target species, and strengthen its resilience 
to environmental change. In this process, the enforcement of the fishing closure will play an 
important role.  
Keywords: Ecosystem modelling, ecosystem-based fisheries management, Ecopath with 
Ecosim, Marine Protected Areas/MPAs 
  
iii 
ZUSAMMENFASSUNG 
Der Golf von Nicoya ist ein äußerst produktives Ästuar an der Pazifikküste Costa Ricas und 
trägt zur wichtigsten Fischproduktion des Landes bei. Er wird zunehmend durch Fischerei, 
Klimaveränderungen, Urbanisierung, touristische Aktivitäten, Verschmutzung aus städtischen 
Zentren und landwirtschaftlichen Aktivitäten in den Einzugsgebieten der großen Flüsse, die in 
den Golf münden, beeinträchtigt.  Unsere Studie untersucht daher im Detail den vergangenen, 
gegenwärtigen und zukünftigen Zustand des Golfs von Nicoya sowie die Hauptfaktoren, die 
das Ökosystem verändern. Analysiert wurden der Zustand des Ökosystems, die Dynamik des 
Nahrungsnetzes, erkennbare Muster im Fischereiaufwand und der Einfluss externer Faktoren 
auf das System. Darüber hinaus wurden die wirtschaftlichen Auswirkungen von 
Fischereiaufwandsregelungen simuliert, um mögliche Managementpläne zu testen und 
vorzuschlagen. Darüber hinaus wurde ein räumliches Modell entwickelt, um die Auswirkungen 
einer saisonalen Einstellung der Fischerei auf die Erholung der Zielartenbestände im Golf von 
Nicoya zu testen und Managementalternativen vorzuschlagen. 
In den frühen 1990er Jahren wurde eine ganzheitliche Analyse ökologischer Informationen und 
Fischereidaten aus dem Golf von Nicoya durchgeführt, auf deren Basis ein trophisches Modell 
mithilfe des Ecopath-Modellierungsansatzes erstellt wurde. Die Resultate legten nahe, dass das 
tropische Ästuar, ein Hotspot der costa-ricanischen Fischerei, bereits durch die 
Schleppnetzfischerei und Langleinenflotten überfischt wurde. Jüngste Beobachtungen deuten 
auf eine weitere Verschlechterung des Zustands des Ökosystems hin. Um die ökosystemischen 
und sozioökonomischen Veränderungen im Golf von Nicoya in den letzten 20 Jahren zu 
bewerten, wurde das Modell von 1993 mit Daten aus dem Jahr 2013 rekonstruiert, sodass ein 
Vergleich beider Systemzustände möglich ist. Die zusammenfassenden Statistiken beider 
Zustände (d. h. 1993 und 2013) lassen den Schluss zu, dass das System seine 
Gesamtfunktionalität beibehielt und sogar einen Anstieg der Fischereifänge verzeichnen 
konnte. Eine detailliertere Analyse der trophischen Ebenen der Funktionsgruppen führte zu dem 
Schluss, dass das System weiter degradiert wurde, was zu einem enormen wirtschaftlichen 
Verlust von etwa 50 % führte. Wie andere Küstenökosysteme wird auch der Golf von Nicoya 
von der Fischerei, anthropogenen Aktivitäten und der Verschmutzung durch die großen Flüsse, 
die in den Golf münden, beeinflusst.  
Daher reagiert der Golf besonders sensibel auf kurz- und langfristige Klimaschwankungen, 
insbesondere während extremer Bedingungen in El-Niño-Perioden.  Im Rahmen der weiteren 
Analyse wurde ein ganzheitlicher Ansatz verfolgt, um die Auswirkungen der Fischerei und der 
Umweltveränderungen zu untersuchen und die Hauptfaktoren für die beobachteten 
Veränderungen im Ökosystem des Golfs von Nicoya zu ermitteln. Die Modellsimulationen 
legen nahe, dass der Fischereiaufwand der stärkste Treiber der Schwankungen ist, wobei 
mehrere Arten ebenfalls erheblich von Klimaschwankungen betroffen sind. Dieser Aspekt muss 
neben der Implementierung von Fischereiverordnungen in die Überlegungen einbezogen 
werden, um adäquate Managementstrategien zu entwickeln und umzusetzen. Im Rahmen dieser 
Dissertation wurde die Effektivität unterschiedlicher Managementstrategien für den Golf von 
Nicoya evaluiert, darunter ein Top-down-Ansatz und eine partizipative Fischereipolitik. Im 
Rahmen der Untersuchung wurde zudem die aktuelle Politik, insbesondere das Verbot der 
iv 
Garnelenschleppnetzfischerei, evaluiert. Dazu wurde das automatische Software-Tool ‘Fishing 
Policy Search‘ der EwE-Software verwendet, um ein alternatives, optimiertes 
Managementszenario zu analysieren. Die durchgeführte Analyse belegt, dass das Verbot der 
Schleppnetzfischerei eine wesentliche Voraussetzung für die Regeneration des Ökosystems 
darstellt. Allerdings könnten zusätzliche Maßnahmen erforderlich sein, beispielsweise eine 
Reduzierung des Fischereiaufwands der semi-industriellen Flotten und der Langleinenflotten. 
Im Folgenden werden zwei alternative Bewirtschaftungsszenarien präsentiert, die sich in ihrem 
Gleichgewicht zwischen der Erhaltung des Ökosystems und den wirtschaftlichen Verlusten 
unterscheiden. Die umfassende Analyse wird durch eine räumliche Komponente ergänzt, wobei 
das Tool Ecospace zum Einsatz kommt. Dieses ermöglicht die Simulation der räumlichen 
Verteilung der Arten sowie des Fischereiaufwands im Laufe der Zeit.  
Die Bewertung der Effizienz bestehender und potenzieller Standorte von Meeresschutzgebieten 
ermöglicht die Untersuchung räumlicher Managementoptionen sowie die Beantwortung 
verschiedener Forschungsfragen, darunter auch die Analyse der regionalen Folgen des 
Klimawandels. Der Ansatz wurde verwendet, um die Effektivität der bestehenden jährlichen 
saisonalen Fischereischließung im Golf (im Spanischen als "Veda" bezeichnet) zu evaluieren 
und weitere Fragestellungen zu beantworten. Beispielsweise wurde untersucht, ob es 
erforderlich ist, alle Flotten während der Fischereischließung aus dem Golf auszuschließen. 
Zudem wurde analysiert, ob eine saisonale Schließung, die das gesamte Gebiet des Golfs von 
Nicoya abdeckt, eine effizientere Maßnahme darstellen würde.  
Die durchgeführte, intensive Bewertung des Zustandes des Ökosystems des Golfs von Nicoya 
führt zu der Schlussfolgerung, dass eine drastische Verringerung des Fischereiaufwands, 
insbesondere der semi-industriellen Flotten, erforderlich ist, um die Funktionsfähigkeit des 
Ökosystems zu erhalten, die Biomasse der Zielarten wieder aufzubauen und die 
Widerstandsfähigkeit des Ökosystems gegenüber Umweltveränderungen zu stärken. Die 
Durchsetzung des Fangverbots spielt in diesem Prozess eine wichtige Rolle.  
Stichworte: Ökosystemmodellierung, Fischereimanagement, Nahrungsnetze, Ecopath with 
Ecosim, Meeresschutzgebiete 
  
v 
RESUMEN 
El Golfo de Nicoya es un estuario sumamente productivo ubicado en la costa del Pacífico de 
Costa Rica y representa la principal producción pesquera del país. Se ve cada vez más afectada 
no solo por su pesca, sino también por las variaciones climáticas, la urbanización, los 
desarrollos turísticos, el ingreso de contaminantes de los centros urbanos y las actividades 
agrícolas en las zonas de captación de grandes ríos que desembocan en el golfo. Por lo tanto, 
nuestro estudio evalúa de manera intensiva los estados pasados, presentes y futuros del Golfo 
de Nicoya y el principal impulsor del cambio en los ecosistemas.   Se analizaron el estado del 
ecosistema, la dinámica de la red alimentaria, los patrones detectables en el esfuerzo pesquero 
y la influencia de los impulsores externos sobre el sistema. Además, se simularon los impactos 
económicos de la regulación del esfuerzo pesquero para probar y proponer posibles planes de 
gestión. También se construyó un modelo espacial para probar el efecto de un cierre temporal 
de la pesca sobre la recuperación de las poblaciones de especies objetivo en el Golfo de Nicoya 
y proponer alternativas de gestión. 
A principios de la década de 1990, se analizaron holísticamente los datos ecológicos y 
pesqueros del Golfo de Nicoya y se construyó un modelo trófico que utiliza el enfoque de 
modelado Ecopath. Los resultados arrojaron que este estuario tropical, que es un punto caliente 
para la pesca costarricense, ya estaba sobreexplotado por los arrastreros camaroneros y las flotas 
palangreras. Asimismo, recientes observaciones sugieren un mayor deterioro en este sistema. 
A fin de evaluar los cambios socioeconómicos y del ecosistema en el Golfo de Nicoya en los 
últimos 20 años, se reconstruyó el modelo de 1993 con datos de 2013 para comparar ambos 
estados del sistema. Si bien las estadísticas resumidas de ambos estados (es decir, 1993 y 2013) 
sugieren que el sistema mantuvo su funcionalidad general e incluso permitió que la cosecha 
total aumentara aproximadamente un 20 %, un análisis más detallado de los niveles de los 
grupos funcionales llevó a la conclusión de que el sistema se degradó aún más, lo que causó 
una enorme pérdida económica de aproximadamente el 50 %. Al igual que otros ecosistemas 
costeros, el Golfo de Nicoya no solo se ve afectado por su pesca, sino también por las 
actividades antropogénicas y la contaminación de los grandes ríos que desembocan en el golfo. 
Por lo tanto, el golfo es particularmente sensible a los cambios climáticos a corto y largo plazo, 
en especial durante las condiciones extremas en los períodos de El Niño. Para un análisis más 
detallado, utilizamos un enfoque holístico que examina tanto los impactos de la pesca como los 
cambios ambientales, e identifica los principales impulsores de los cambios observados en el 
ecosistema del Golfo de Nicoya. Si bien las simulaciones de modelos indican que las 
variaciones en la cuenca son impulsadas en especial por las tasas de explotación pesquera, 
varias especies también se ven sustancialmente afectadas por las variaciones climáticas. A fin 
de formar y desarrollar estrategias de gestión adecuadas, este factor debe considerarse junto con 
la implementación de la regulación pesquera. En esta disertación, probamos la efectividad de 
diferentes situaciones de gestión aplicados al Golfo de Nicoya, incluidas las políticas de pesca 
tanto de arriba hacia abajo como participativas. Además de probar la política actual (que 
prohíbe la pesca de arrastre de camarón), utilizamos la herramienta de búsqueda automatizada 
de políticas pesqueras del software EwE para explorar una situación hipotética de gestión 
alternativa y optimizada. El análisis indica que la prohibición de la pesca de arrastre es una 
medida importante para facilitar la recuperación del ecosistema. Sin embargo, podrían 
vi 
necesitarse algunas medidas adicionales, como la reducción del esfuerzo pesquero de las flotas 
de cerco semiindustriales y de las flotas de palangre artesanales. Presentamos dos posibles 
situaciones de gestión alternativas, con diferentes equilibrios de conservación del ecosistema y 
pérdidas económicas. Complementamos nuestro extenso análisis agregando un componente 
espacial mediante la herramienta Ecospace, que puede utilizarse para la simulación de la 
distribución espacial de las especies y el esfuerzo de la flota pesquera a lo largo del tiempo. 
Una aplicación obvia es la evaluación de la eficiencia de las ubicaciones existentes y potenciales 
de las AMP para explorar opciones de gestión espacial y abordar una variedad de cuestiones de 
investigación, incluidas las consecuencias regionales del cambio climático. Este enfoque se 
utilizó para probar la eficacia de la actual veda anual de pesca estacional en el golfo (conocida 
como “veda”) y también abordar otras cuestiones. Por ejemplo, ¿es necesario excluir todas las 
flotas del golfo durante el cierre de la pesca? ¿Sería más eficiente un cierre estacional que cubra 
toda el área del Golfo de Nicoya?  
Esta evaluación intensiva del estado del ecosistema del Golfo de Nicoya lleva a la conclusión 
de que se necesita disminuir drásticamente el esfuerzo pesquero, en especial de las flotas 
semiindustriales, con el fin de mantener la funcionalidad del ecosistema y reconstruir la 
biomasa de las especies objetivo y fortalecer su resiliencia al cambio ambiental. En este 
proceso, la aplicación del cierre de la pesca desempeñará un papel importante.  
Palabras clave: modelado de ecosistemas, gestión pesquera basada en ecosistemas, Ecopath 
con Ecosim, Áreas Marinas Protegidas/AMP 
 
  
vii

journey was accompanied by my very patient and committed supervisor Prof. Dr. Matthias 
Wolff. The beginning of my PhD position was marked by the research stay in San Jose, Costa 
Rica, where Matthias Wolff introduced me to his long-standing colleagues and partners at the 
CIMAR and University of Costa Rica. Especially his former colleague Prof. Dr. Alvaro Morales 
took the lead to supervise me at my research stay in Costa Rica. Thanks to the CIMAR, my host 
Alvaro Morales and his colleagues. As well as to the INCOPESCA for working with me and 
sharing some valuable information and data with me. 
After my fruitful stay in San José, I returned to the ZMT Bremen and was lucky to have the 
chance to learn a lot from Matthias Wolff and the whole research group. I thank Matthias Wolff 
for accompanying me and my work over the years including a long period of absence due to 
the maternity leave with my three kids. Matthias Wolf is a very engaged supervisor, who is 
always available for advice. I also thank my former working group colleagues at the ZMT. 
A very special thanks goes out to our Postdoc researcher Dr. Giovanni Romagnoni who joined 
us in 2021. He helped me to get back on track during and after my maternity leave, kept me 
updated and motivated throughout a time of home office, the covid pandemic and a lack of 
childcare. He has the ability to motivate people in a very friendly way and made mobilize all 
my strength, to work flexible in the late evenings and on the weekends to finish my third 
publication. Not to forget, that I was able to learn a lot from him about Ecopath, Ecosim and 
Ecospace improving the quality of my research. Giovanni was always available to help and had 
a good advice for every problem on hand. I thank Matthias and Giovanni for adding their 
valuable input and ideas to my research and helping me improve the publications through their 
co-authoring. 
I also would like to thank my husband and my family for their support and understanding. 
Especially during times of paper submission and in the last weeks of my PhD I could count on 
your support.

GENERAL INTRODUCTION 
  
2 
1.1 Small-Scale Fisheries 
1.1.1 Small-Scale Fisheries in a Global Context 
Small-scale fisheries (SSFs) support approximately 90% of all fishery jobs worldwide, with 
around 32 million people employed as small-scale fishers and an additional 76 million in post-
harvest jobs (Bennett et al. 2020; Kelleher et al. 2012) . SSFs produce more than half of all 
fishery landings in developing nations (Lozano and Heinen 2015), whereas 81% of the catch is 
used for local human consumption. Although the SSFs vary substantially by region, some 
characteristics are common: their use of small vessels and engines; simple or more traditional 
gears; small crews; their proximity to the coast, family or local ownership; and their importance 
for local livelihoods and subsistence (Bennett et al. 2020; Kittinger et al. 2013; Smith and 
Basurto 2019). The small-scale artisanal fishing sector is highly socioeconomically important 
for securing food production and livelihoods in coastal communities and has additional 
economic benefits to many countries. 
There is increasing recognition of the importance of sustaining SSFs within fishers’ 
management and development policies. Most studies of SSFs in developing countries in the last 
25 years have emphasized small-scale fisherfolks’ resource dependence and the open-access 
nature of fisheries that lead to resource degradation, poverty, and marginalization (Arthur 2020; 
Kalikoski et al. 2019). The interest in the ‘sustainable livelihoods approach’ has continuously 
increased. This approach aims to reduce poverty in low-income countries by understanding the 
strategies of artisanal fishing communities affected by variations in fishery resources (Allison 
and Ellis 2001). The aim is to find management directives that combine resource conservation 
with social and economic goals. Intensifying fishing pressures often lead to increasing 
competition between industrial fisheries and SSFs, which is at an increased disadvantage to 
SSFs (Salas et al. 2011), as well as conflicts among artisanal and industrial fishing sectors and 
governmental agencies. This is a common problem in different marine regions of the world. In 
West African waters, for example, the government responded to the depletion of marine 
resources with measures that mainly targeted small-scale fishers, resulting in the 
marginalization and increased vulnerability of SSF (Okafor-Yarwood et al. 2022). The marine 
sector of Bangladesh faces similar problems. Here, the number of artisanal fishers is 
continuously decreasing due to the increasing dominance of commercial fishers and the strong 
competition during peak fishing periods (Alam et al. 2021). In the Mediterranean, there is 
increasing competition between artisanal and industrial fishing operations and recreational 
fisheries, changing the role of coastal fisheries in some European countries (Lloret et al. 2018). 
To add complexity to the situation, there is typically a lack of quantitative data and ecosystem 
information, which would help to avoid unexpected trophic dynamics in the system. 
Consequently, managing artisanal fisheries is challenging (Batista et al. 2014). Despite the 
complex situation and lack of information, countermeasures should be taken against the 
ongoing depletion of coastal waters, often leading to top-down regulations (Lozano and Heinen 
2016). However, the top-down enforced management rules usually fail either due to 
noncompliance of fishers or the lack of control capacities. Besides finding the optimal balance 
between different management goals for successful policy-making, it can be a good approach 
to consider the interests and knowledge of local stakeholders (Wilson et al. 2006). Co-
3 
management is an alternative approach to effective management. It is defined as sharing 
responsibility and authority between the state and resource users. It fosters stakeholder 
collaboration, including government agencies, non-governmental organizations, research 
organizations, private enterprises, and civil society (Carlsson and Berkes 2005; Evans 2010). It 
is suggested that co-management, which can improve social participation and compliance with 
the fishery’s management rules of all actors (d’Armengol et al. 2018), while it reduces conflicts, 
fosters responsibility among resource users (Nielsen and Vedsmand 1999), and reduces 
management costs (Carlsson and Berkes 2005). There are several successful examples of 
fisheries where co-management has been practiced, mostly in coastal multispecies fisheries 
(d’Armengol et al. 2018), also from Latin America. 
1.1.2 SSFs in the Case of Costa Rica’s Fishing Hotspot 
Latin American coastal waters are highly productive and support essential fisheries worldwide; 
thus, these waters have high economic value (Blaber 2002) and provide livelihoods for many 
coastal inhabitants (Figure 1.1).  Coastal ecosystems often serve as important nursery, feeding, 
or reproduction grounds for many commercially important fish and invertebrates (Abrantes et 
al. 2015).  Especially, mangrove forests provide organic matter and shelter and contribute to 
enhancing fish and invertebrate productivity; thus, mangroves strongly support the local 
fisheries (Wolff 2006; Hutchison et al. 2014). 
  
Figure 1.1 - Map of the total annual catches (t km-2) in 2019 in the Eastern Pacific Ocean, Caribbean And Atlantic 
Ocean, displaying the density of catches sin coastal regions. From the global databases (‘Sea Around Us’ 2024). 
The marine fishing sector in Costa Rica has steadily grown in the last decades, advancing its 
gear and boats, which have increased its fishing range and catch effectiveness. In Costa Rica, 
around 80% of landings come from artisanal fleets, most of which operate in the Pacific Ocean 
(Salas et al. 2011). The Gulf of Nicoya has an exceptionally high productivity, which makes it 
Costa Rica’s principal fishing ground for the artisanal fishing fleet and where its highest 
landings are concentrated (Salas et al. 2011). This makes the Gulf of Nicoya one of Central 
America’s largest and most exploited estuaries, where many stocks have already been over-

4 
exploited and/or collapsed. According to the Sea Around Us statistics (‘Sea Around Us’ 2024), 
almost 90% of the fish stocks in the Gulf of Nicoya are exploited, 80% are over-exploited, and 
40% have already collapsed (Figure 1.2). Thus, the Gulf of Nicoya is a suitable example of a 
region facing overexploitation and increasing conflicts among fishing sectors due to decreasing 
resources (Lozano and Heinen 2016a). 
 
Figure 1.2 - Time series of number (%) of fish stocks by status in the Gulf of Nicoya from 1950 to 2020 (the fish 
stock red: collapsed, orange: is overexploited, beige: is exploited, light green: is developing or dark green: is 
rebuilding). The figure is derived from (‘Sea Around Us’ 2024) 
The Gulf of Nicoya is tremendously important to artisanal fishermen, sustaining their 
livelihoods and maintaining food production security. SSFs are historically rooted in the 
communities surrounding the gulf, for which fishing is the primary source of income, making 
them especially vulnerable to overexploitation (Carrillo et al. 2019). In Costa Rice, the artisanal 
fishing sector dominates, providing livelihoods for coastal communities, and many fishermen 
fish for subsistence. The fisheries market is relatively small, and the commercial fisheries 
represent around 2-3 % of the GDP (Sabau 2017; Salas et al. 2011) . However, the advanced 
artisanal and semi-industrial fleet focuses their production on the export market, with species 
such as tuna, mahi mahi (Dolphinfish), marlin, sharks, and shrimps. Small pelagic fish are sold 
to the commercial fisheries as bait. 
To protect marine resources and to ensure a more sustainable use, some measures on the 
regional scale have already been implemented. The measures of input control include fishing 
effort limitation through granting licenses and a seasonal closure known as the “Veda” to all 
fishing fleets, as well as the technical limitation of gear size (mesh sizes >6.35 cm [2.5 in]). 
However, most of these top-down enforced management rules failed due to the fishers’ non-
compliance or the lack of control capacities. These fishing regulations have been supplemented 
with more severe enforcement, such as the ban on shrimp trawling for the entire Gulf of Nicoya. 
In 2013, semi-industrial shrimp trawling was declared unconstitutional in Costa Rica, and no 
further licenses have been issued since 2015. The last remaining shrimp trawling license in the 
Gulf of Nicoya expired in 2019. This was a major step towards a more sustainable fishing 
industry and significantly reduced fishing efforts.  
Costa Rica is a model region that can guide many other coastal areas worldwide. On the one 
hand, it has an immense potential for marine conservation, with an Exclusive Economic Zone 
(EEZ) ten times larger than its terrestrial area. On the other hand, the country has already proven 
its understanding of the urgency of protecting its environment, aiming to be among the global 
leaders in sustainability and environmental protection (UNEP, 2019). More than ever, 

5 
significant attention is being paid to global marine conservation. Like Costa Rica, many 
countries are interested in pushing for ocean conservation, for example, by joining the Decade 
of Ocean Sciences for Sustainable Development (2021–2030) proclaimed by the United 
Nations. The goal is to achieve a healthy, resilient, and sustainably harvested ocean for many 
coastal countries worldwide (Heymans et al. 2020; Ryabinin et al. 2019).   
1.1.3 Description of the Study Area 
The Gulf of Nicoya (10°N, 85°W) is a tidally-influenced estuary along the northwest Pacific 
coast of Costa Rica. It is one of the world’s most productive tropical estuaries. It stretches from 
the northern inland (at the mouth of the Tempisque River) to the entrance of the Pacific Ocean 
in the south and covers an area of 1530 km2. According to its shape and bathymetry, the Gulf 
of Nicoya can be divided into the following three regions: inner gulf, intermediate gulf, and 
outer gulf, named zones 201, 202, and 203, respectively, by the Costa Rican Institute of 
Fisheries (INCOPESCA). The division is based on species composition and the fishing gear 
(Sánchez-Jiménez et al. 2019). 
 The shallow interior zone (depth<10 m) is fringed by mangroves and mudflats and is 
dominated by the seasonal freshwater inflow from the Tempisque River (Proyecto Golfos 
2012). The inner gulf is considered an area of high priority by the Instituto Costarricense de 
Pesca y Acuicultura (INCOPESCA) due to its vital function as a reproduction and nursery area 
for commercially important fish and shrimp species (Alpí zar 2011; Proyecto Golfos 2012; 
Vargas 2016). Sandy beaches and mangroves mostly surround the intermediate zone of the gulf. 
The physical parameters of the water masses are subject to intense mixing processes due to the 
interaction between the oceanic water and fresh water from both adjacent zones (Proyecto 
Golfos 2012). The region comprises reproduction areas for small pelagic fish species 
(Clupeiformes:  Clupeidae and Engraulidae), shrimp Litopenaeus species (Decapoda: 
Penaeidae), snapper Lutjanus species (Perciformes: Lutjanidae), and drum Cynoscion species 
(Perciformes:  Sciaenidae) and serves as a feeding ground for several commercially targeted 
species, such as the Mexican Barracuda (Perciformes: Sphyraena). The outer oceanic zone 
extends from a minimum depth of 20 m out to 200 m at the shelf edge, is surrounded by sandy 
6 
beaches and rocky shores (Wolff et al.1998), and is mainly influenced by oceanic waters and 
the input from the Tarcoles River. 
 
 
Figure 1.3 - Maps of the study area (Gulf of Nicoya, Costa Rica) including the three fishing zones (i.e., inner gulf, 
zone 201; intermediate gulf, zone 202; and outer gulf, zone 203) . 
The small-scale artisanal fishing sector is of primary economic and social importance for 
approximately 2000 fishermen and more than 20 coastal communities (Lozano and Heinen 
2016a; Trujillo et al. 2012). Four artisanal fishing fleets operate in the gulf with different boat 
sizes and gear types that target various demersal and coastal species (BIOMARCC-SINAC-
GIZ 2013a). Gillnets are predominantly used, followed by the longline, handline, and manual 
mollusk extraction combined with the manual trap fisheries targeting different crustaceans, the 
latter mostly carried out by women from the coastal communities. The main port of the Gulf of 
Nicoya, Puntarenas, is also the landing site for semi-industrial fishing fleets such as the purse 
seine fleet and the shrimp trawl fleet. The purse seine vessels target small pelagic fish species, 
such as tropical sardines and herrings (Family Clupeidae), Anchovy and Pacific anchoveta 
(Anchoa spp. and Cetengraulis mysticetus)  in the intermediate and outer zones of the Gulf of 
Nicoya. Before the trawling ban, the bottom trawl fleet targeted several shrimp species, mainly 
in the outer gulf; however, the capture of bycatch species drastically exceeded the shrimp 
catches (Clarke et al. 2016).  
 
1.2 A Case Example of Holistic Ecosystem Management 
1.2.1 Ecosystem Approach to Fisheries 
Changes in the biomass that occur at one trophic level are known to affect the trophic levels 
above and below that level, causing trophic cascades throughout the system (e.g., Pace et al. 
1999; Duarte and García 2004; Andersen and Pedersen 2009). Hence, th e analysis of changes 
in the ecosystem over time can be particularly useful for explaining changes in trophic 
relationships (Coll et al. 2009) and changes in the effort allocation and catches of the fishing 
fleets due to indirect conflicts and competition for resources (Bastardie et al. 2015). 

7 
Ecosystem food webs change over time due to changes in natural predator-prey interactions, 
resource limitations, anthropogenic influences, and environmental influences (Jennings and 
Kaiser 1998; Frederiksen et al. 2006). Fishing, as a substantial anthropog enic impact factor, 
greatly affects the population size of target species, their prey, and predators and may even 
damage the species’ habitats (Frederiksen et al. 2006; Jennings and Kaiser 1998). Although 
overfishing is known to have caused the decline and collapse of many fish stocks worldwide, it 
can also alter the sensitivity of the ecosystem to natural environmental variations. It may reduce 
the ability to buffer the effects of extreme natural events or future climate change (Hidalgo et 
al. 2011). Thus, since ecosystems are driven by the combined impact of varied influencing 
factors, these must be identified and examined to understand the system’s dynamics. In our 
study region, we also considered the El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) cycle that causes 
periodic variations in several environmental parameters, which temporarily change the coastal 
marine environment and its species composition (Fiedler 2002; Tam et al. 2008; Taylor et al. 
2008b; Wolff et al. 2012). This phenomenon is relevant for the Eastern Tropical Pacific (ETP) 
region and other marine regions worldwide (Bacalso et al. 2023; Del Solar et al. 2024).  
In many marine areas worldwide, ecological information is needed to achieve the goals of the 
The Decade of Ocean Science for Sustainable Development’ (2021–2030) proclaimed by the 
United Nations (UN). To achieve a healthy, resilient, and sustainably harvested ocean 
(Heymans et al. 2020; Ryabinin et al. 2019), the current High Seas Treaty aims to convert 30% 
of the seas into protected areas by 2030 (United Nations). In many regions of the world, suitable 
areas for protection need to be identified. However, for the efficiency of MPAs, the right choice 
of location, the spatial and temporal extent, and the successful enforcement are crucial 
(Dimarchopoulou et al. 2024). 
Therefore, the need for suitable and successful management policies, plans, and science-based 
decisions is greater than ever. Thus, this study presents a case example of the Ecosystem 
Approach to Fisheries (EAF) (FAO 2002) as a possible way to provide the necessary 
information for management objectives. The EAF considers the trophic interactions among 
functional groups within the ecosystem and includes environmental, ecological, and 
anthropogenic impacts on an ecosystem in the management consideration. Following the EAF, 
one can find optimal, sustainable exploitation rates under the constraints of biodiversity 
conservation and an increase in the ecosystem’s health and/or in the rebuilding stocks of target 
species (Forrest et al. 2015). The EAF provides clear benefits compared to single-species 
management approaches, which are often insufficient (Cochrane 2002; FAO 2012).  
In the context of the EAF, we constructed a holistic ecosystem model of the Gulf of Nicoya 
(Alms and Wolff 2019), developed with the open-source software Ecopath with Ecospace to 
study the effects of fishing across the system.  Ecosystem and multispecies models are vital to 
approaching and exploring ecosystem-related questions. More than 450 EwE ecosystem models 
of aquatic systems have been published, and our procedure could be applied to many of these 
models. We develop and apply a set of models with the spectrum of the EwE software, starting 
with a mass-balanced food web model, continuing with dynamic time-series modelling, and 
finishing with a spatial model. In addition, we apply an automated fishing policy search tool 
that looks for future developments/system changes under certain management plans. We 
investigated different management scenarios while considering different management 
8 
objectives or a combination (Christensen and Walters 2004) by optimizing for socioeconomic 
and/or ecosystem conservation goals. We also holistically use this information to present a 
spatial ecosystem model of the Gulf of Nicoya. This model displays fishing effort and species 
distribution, which can be used to validate the existing MPAs’ performance and to find potential 
areas suitable for additional protection.  
By 2020, 7.74% of marine areas worldwide were conserved through protected areas and other 
effective area-based conservation measures (UNEP-WCMC and IUCN 2021). In 2010, the 
United Nations set a goal to preserve 10% of the ocean by 2020. The recent High Seas Treaty 
even aims to place 30% of the seas into protected areas by 2030 to conserve marine nature 
(Johanna J Heymans et al. 2020). In many regions of the world, Ecospace can be a valuable 
tool for finding suitable areas for protection. 
1.2.2 Technical Approach: Ecopath with Ecosim Software 
1.2.2.1 Ecopath - Static model/mass-balanced model 
For the dissertation, we encompassed the full spectrum of the Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) 
software, starting with a static model (Ecopath), continuing with time-series modelling and 
policy optimization (Ecosim), and finishing with temporal-spatial modelling (Ecospace).  
The Ecopath approach has been widely used to assess and visualize structural changes in a 
given ecosystem (Pauly, Christensen, and Walters 2000). The open-source software Ecopath 
with Ecosim (EwE) versions 6.4.4 to 6.6.5 (Christensen and Walters 2004) was used for the 
modelling task. Ecopath with Ecosim is based on a set of linear equations for each functional 
group in the system through which the group's biomass production is connected to the other 
model groups via a prey–predator matrix. For each compartment ( i), the basic equation is as 
follows: 
Pi = Yi + BiM2i + Ei + Bai + Pi(1-EE) 
where Pi is the total production rate of i; Y i is the total fishery catch rate; M2 i is the 
instantaneous predation rate of group i; Bi is the biomass; E i is the net migration rate 
(emigration–immigration); BA i is the biomass accumulation rate; P i(1 − EE) represents the 
“other mortality” rate of i; and EE is the ecotrophic efficiency of i and the proportion of the 
production that is utilized in the system (Christensen and Walters 2004; Christensen et al. 2005). 
Although equation 1 allows for the balancing of the biomass production in each group with its 
use in the system (and the fishery), a second equation provides for estimating the energy budget 
of each group i as follows: 
Consumptioni = Productioni + Respirationi + Egestion of unassimilated foodi 
The model was built around the main groups targeted by the fisheries. Species with similar 
sizes, diets, consumption, mortality, production rates, and habitats were grouped. The model 
comprised 22 functional groups, including three primary producer groups and ten different fish 
groups.  
In the first article presented in this thesis (Chapter 2), two mass-balanced snapshots of the Gulf 
of Nicoya ecosystem have been produced, comprising the same ecosystem components at two 
9 
distinct time points (1993 and 2013) to reveal changes in the food web of the marine community 
(Neira, Arancibia, and Cubillos 2004). For this analysis, we determined the differences in 
biomass distribution, system flow structure, and system summary statistics for all system 
components, including primary producers, target species, and nontarget species. By applying 
the mixed trophic impact (MTI) analysis in Ecopath, we assessed the effect of the different 
fishing fleets and gear types on the marine community and the interactions among the species 
and the identified keystone species (Libralato et al. 2006) in both system states (i.e., 1993 and 
2013). Assessing keystone species can be crucial for ecosystem management, as resource 
conservation plans often focus more on species of high commercial value than those of high 
ecological value (Navia et al. 2016). In addition, we used the Ecopath software to calculate the 
economic profit of the fisheries during both periods. Information regarding the resource market 
prices and the fishing costs (variable and fixed costs) for each of the fishing fleets was included 
in the Ecopath models (Alms and Wolff 2019).  
1.2.2.2 Ecosim - Time-series fitting 
Ecosim can incorporate time series data, which can be used to test the model performance and 
explore policy options for ecosystem-based fisheries management (Christensen and Steenbeek 
2024). Forcing data (fishing effort or fishing mortality) is needed to trace the biomass changes 
of the model compartments over time, and reference data is necessary for comparison and 
validation of the models. In our case, fishing effort (days fishing) and time series data of the 
environmental parameters were used to force model group catches and biomasses over time, 
whereas the recorded historic landings served as (observational) reference data for the catch. 
During time-series fitting, a statistical measure of goodness of fit to these data is generated in 
Ecosim as a weighted sum of squared deviations (SS) of log biomasses from log predicted 
biomasses. The goodness of fit indicates how the model performs and whether it can be used 
for policy exploration (Christensen and Steenbeek 2024). Ecosim can be used to identify 
ecosystem effects of fishing and environmental factors (Christensen and Steenbeek 2024). 
In the second article included in this thesis (Chapter 3), we present time-series models based on 
the two EwE “snapshot models” of the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem. The Ecosim time-series 
fitting routine was applied (Christensen and Steenbeek 2024), using fisheries and 
environmental data from 1994 to 2014 to elucidate the role of the fishing effort and abiotic 
factors in the ecosystem. A hypothesis-based model fitting procedure was applied, for which 
time series of inter-annual changes in fishing effort, fleet composition, and the most relevant 
abiotic factors were considered. Three essential classes of ecosystem drivers were tested: 
anthropogenic factors (fishing, pollution), trophic control mechanisms (interactions), and the 
influence of the chemical and physical environment (climate). The procedure is an example of 
examining whether observed system changes are driven by the exploitation patterns of fisheries 
and/or by environmental changes. This is a central question relevant to many other ecosystems 
and has been applied in similar systems, e.g., the Bering Sea, the Tropics, and the Mediterranean 
(Coll et al. 2016; Lam et al. 2020; Mackinson et al. 2009; Punt et al. 2024) . We consider 
identifying the main drivers behind the observed changes as crucial requisites for selecting 
appropriate management and conservation strategies. 
Ecosim implements the ‘Fishing policy exploration’ tool in EwE 6.6.5 (Christensen and 
Steenbeek 2024). This optimization method can be used to search for fishing policies that 
10 
maximize particular policy goals for management. The policy exploration has two alternative 
objective functions, where by default, the objective function is defined based on an evaluation 
of the weighted policy objectives that maximize profits or net economic value (value of the 
catch minus costs of fishing), social benefits /jobs provided by the fishing fleets), biodiversity 
(biomass distribution across functional groups), ecosystem structure (Ecosystem ‘health’) 
(Christensen and Walters 2024). 
In the third article (Chapter 4), we present the application of the policy exploration tool to find 
the optimal management policy for the Gulf of Nicoya. We used a scenario-based approach 
using the EwE simulation tool. We tested the losses and benefits of the social and economic 
value (profit and number of jobs) and the conservational criteria (ecosystem health and 
biodiversity) caused by different management scenarios after 20 years. Furthermore, the fishing 
policy tool was applied to explore alternative management scenarios. The automated search 
routine allows the assessment of appropriate management scenarios while considering different 
management objectives or a combination of them (Christensen and Walters 2004). The output 
can, for example, be optimized for socioeconomic and ecosystem conservation goals. It can 
distinguish between a project with long-term benefits versus a project with short-term benefits 
and long-term costs. Thus, the fishing policy search routine can be beneficial for different 
scientific questions while exploring different management strategies. One of these scenarios is 
a stakeholder-based policy, whereby gillnet fleet efforts are reduced by 25% and aimed at 
allowing high trophic level species to rebuild their stock biomass. 
 In an interdisciplinary collaboration with social scientists, a participatory management 
workshop was developed in Costa Rica (Sánchez-Jiménez et al. 2019; Sánchez Jiménez et al. 
2021). In these workshops, stakeholders were invited, and ecological information about the 
Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem was presented using the EwE modelling software. The fishermen 
suggested possible policy plans based on the visualization and information about ecosystem 
health and future perspectives. The stakeholder-based policy was developed to encourage 
stakeholder cooperation and exchange and foster participatory management in the Gulf of 
Nicoya. In addition to finding the optimal balance between different management goals to 
create successful policies, it is vital to consider the interests and knowledge of local 
stakeholders, thereby increasing the compliance of all actors (Wilson et al. 2006). Examples 
show that endeavors to move from top-down management towards co-management can 
successfully enhance learning and knowledge among stakeholders and improve the relationship 
and trust between stakeholders. It may ensure the well-being of the fishers and the fishery, e.g., 
the coastal fisheries of Uruguay and Baja California in Mexico. (Cota-Nieto et al. 2018; Trimble 
and Berkes 2013). 
1.2.2.3 Ecospace – Spatial model 
Ecospace is a modelling tool that can be applied to a broad range of research questions that aim 
to incorporate a spatial component (Christensen and Steenbeek 2024). It can simulate the spatial 
distribution of species and fishing fleet effort over time, explore spatial management options, 
and address various research questions, including the regional consequences of climate change 
(Christensen and Walters 2004). The temporal and spatial dynamic of Ecospace models has, in 
many cases, contributed to Ecosystem-Based Fisheries Management (Christensen and Walters 
2024), e.g., in the EEZ of the Azores, the Gulf of Mexico, and the North Sea (Brito et al. 2023; 
11 
O’Farrell et al. 2017; Romagnoni et al. 2015). Moreover, the location and efficiency of existing 
MPAs and optimal locations for new protected areas can be assessed.  
In the fourth manuscript (Chapter 5), we present how we use the Ecospace maps to asses and 
display species and effort distribution in a period of exploitation by shrimp trawlers and the 
period after the ban of trawling. We model the effects of decreasing fishing pressure on shrimp 
species and several bycatch species and a long-term effect on the upper trophic levels. 
Additionally, we assessed the impact of the seasonal fishing closure in the Gulf of Nicoya and 
the consequences of changes (relaxation of regulations) in the MPA regulations due to the 
COVID-19 pandemic. 
 
1.3 Scope of the Thesis 
This thesis’ main subject involves analyzing the past, present, and future situations/state of the 
Gulf of Nicoya as a fishing ground of economic relevance, a natural habitat for many marine 
and coastal species, and a living environment and livelihood for many coastal communities. It 
allows for the comparison over a long period. The analysis includes important aspects for an 
EAF, including food-web modelling, time-series fitting, economic validation, spatial 
modelling, stakeholder analysis, engagement, and fisheries management/evaluation of 
management policy.  
This PhD thesis incorporates the practical handling of the EwE software by applying its 
different tools to the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem model and interpreting the modelling results. 
This procedure is replicable and can be transferred to other existing or newly-built EwE models 
in various areas of the world. The thesis presents the wide range of ecological and economic 
information that can be gathered based on a solid ecosystem model using EwE as a sole 
instrument. It highlights that the capability and potential of EwE software go far beyond 
creating food-web models. The EwE software is a powerful tool that adds economic and social 
information to the assessment, often lacking in ecological studies. Spatial analysis and 
visualization can foster participatory management and stimulate stakeholder discussion. 
12 
 
  
THE PAST OF THE GULF OF NICOYA ECOSYSTEM: 
TWO DECADES OF CHANGE ECOPATH  
13 
Chapter 2 
 
The Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) Fisheries System: Two Decades of Change. 
Viola Alms and Matthias Wolff

decades of change. Marine and Coastal Fisheries, 11, 139–161. 
https://doi.org/10.1002/mcf2.10050

14 
Abstract 
In the early 1990s, ecological and ﬁshery data from the Gulf of Nicoya (Paciﬁc Ocean) were 
holistically analyzed, and a trophic model was constructed using the Ecopath modeling 
approach. The results indicated that this tropical estuary, which is a Costa Rican ﬁshery hot 
spot, was already overexploited by shrimp trawlers and the longline ﬂeet, and recent 
observations suggest further deteriorations in this system. To evaluate the ecosystem and 
socioeconomic changes in the Gulf of Nicoya over the last 20 years, the 1993 model was 
reconstructed with data from 2013 to compare both system states. Although the summary 
statistics of both states (i.e., 1993 and 2013) suggested that the system maintained its general 
functionality and even enabled the total harvest to increase by approximately 20%, a more 
detailed analysis of the levels of the functional groups suggested that the system was further 
degraded as follows: (1) most shrimp, eel, and catﬁsh species decreased their share in the 
catches, with severe economic losses for the ﬁshery;  (2) of the species that substantially 
increased their share in the catches, most were short-lived, low-value species, such as small 
pelagic and small demersal ﬁshes; and (3) catches of long -lived, high-trophic-level ﬁsh, such 
as rays, sharks, mackerels, and barracudas, decreased. A picture emerged in which the 
advancing ﬁshery of low-trophic-level species with low economic value enabled the total 
harvest to increase, while valuable shrimp resources and higher-trophic-level species were 
depleted. These developments caused a tremendous economic loss of approximately 50%. 
Keywords: Ecosystem modelling, , Ecopath with Ecosim, trophic model, estuary 
  
15 
2.1 Introduction 
Coastal waters are often highly productive and support essential ﬁsheries worldwide; thus, these 
waters have a high economic value (Blaber et al., 2000). The vast majority of Costa Rican 
ﬁshery catches originate from the country’s relatively large Exclusive Economic Zone (10 times 
the terrestrial area) along the Paciﬁc coast, a region that is known to be nutrient-rich and highly 
productive, and most of its target ﬁsh stocks are known to be heavily exploited (Heileman 2012; 
Sea Around Us Project. 2014). Costa Rica’s principal ﬁshing ﬂeets concentrate in the Gulf of 
Nicoya, which accounts for a large proportion of the national catches and is among the most 
exploited coastal areas in Central America (Guzmán-Mora and Molina-Ureña 2008). The small-
scale artisanal ﬁshing sector is historically rooted in the communities surrounding the Gulf of 
Nicoya, is highly socioeconomically important for securing food production and livelihoods, 
and has national economic beneﬁts. Initially, the artisanal ﬁshing activities were conﬁned to the 
inner gulf and shallow waters (Charles and Herrera-Ulloa 1994), but the ﬂeets progressively 
extended to pelagic, offshore, and deepwater areas in the gulf (BIOMARCC-SINAC-GIZ 
2013b). 
After a period of intense exploitation by the multi-gear artisanal ﬂeet and small industrial ﬂeets, 
which peaked in the early 2000s, catches of several commercially important species began to 
show clear signs of decline and are likely to follow the same downward trend as the catches of 
many other ﬁsh stocks in the Paciﬁc Central American Large Marine Ecosystem (Heileman 
2012; Sea Around Us Project. 2014). In particular, coastal shrimp catches drastically declined, 
with a severe reduction of white shrimps Litopenaeus sp. (Trujillo et al. 2012). These 
developments could lead to severe ecological and socioeconomic consequences because 
penaeid shrimps are known to be a central food source in the Gulf of Nicoya’s trophic web 
(Wolff et al. 1998) and are the most sought-after target group by many local coastal 
communities, which are highly vulnerable to ﬂuctuations and negative trends in the ﬁshery 
catches (Bakun 1999). Coastal demersal ﬁsh, which dominate the catches of many inshore 
artisanal ﬁsheries in the tropical Paciﬁc, are valuable marine resources and are also commonly 
overexploited (Bell et al. 2011; Trujillo et al. 2012). Similarly, in the Gulf of Nicoya, large- and 
medium-sized drums and croakers (Perciformes: Sciaenidae) have always constituted a 
substantial and proﬁtable proportion of the artisanal catches, but currently they are caught less 
frequently. The decline of high-value coastal species in the Gulf of Nicoya has caused (1) the 
semi-industrial ﬂeet effort to shift toward other, low-value target groups, such as small pelagic 
ﬁsh species; and (2) the artisanal effort to shift toward smaller -sized individuals and juveniles 
due to the augmented use of small illegal mesh sizes (6.35 cm [2.5 in]). 
These developments have likely caused changes in the size and species composition within the 
functional groups. Hence, an updated analysis of the species and weight composition of catches 
appears to be important for the evaluation of ecological changes in the system and the associated 
socioeconomic consequences (Roessig et al. 2004; Ward and Myers 2005a) . The depletion of 
coastal resources is not only associated with economic losses for the semi-industrial sector but 
is also likely to threaten the livelihoods of the artisanal ﬁshing communities surrounding the 
Gulf of Nicoya. Hence, there is an urgent need for an ecosystem-based ﬁshery assessment of 
the gulf to provide a decision-making basis for the Costa Rican management authorities. The 
Gulf of Nicoya has been studied by many scientists (Vargas 1995; Vargas 2016) , but ﬁshery 
16 
studies concerning commercially exploited species within the area are sparse. Since the trophic 
model of the Gulf of Nicoya was constructed by Wolff et al. (1998), no further attempts have 
been made to integrate ﬁshery-relevant data in a holistic, ecosystem-based analysis. Since that 
model’s construction in the early 1990s, important resources in the system have already been 
considered overexploited, and a further degradation of the ecosystem has been predicted (Wolff 
et al. 1998). We attempted to rebuild and update the model using data obtained approximately 
20 years later from collaborative studies that were complemented by additional information 
from the literature regarding catches of target resources to analyze the ecosystem changes that 
occurred over the two decades between 1993 and 2013. 
Changes in the biomass that occur at one trophic level are known to affect the trophic levels 
above and below that level, causing trophic cascades throughout the system (Pace et al. 1999; 
Andersen and Pedersen 2010; Duarte and Garcı́a 2004). Hence, the analysis of changes in the 
ecosystem over time can be particularly useful for explaining changes in trophic relationships 
(Coll et al. 2009)and for determining the effects of certain ﬁshing regimes on the trophic 
structure of the ecosystem (e.g. Neira et al., 2014; Walters et al., 1997) . Thus, we used an 
Ecopath approach to produce mass-balanced snapshots of the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem 
comprising the same ecosystem components at two distinct time points (1993 and 2013) to 
reveal the changes in the food web of the marine community (Christensen and Walters 2004a). 
For this analysis, we determined the differences in the biomass distribution, system ﬂow 
structure, and system summary statistics for all system components, including primary 
producers, target species, and nontarget species (Gascuel et al. 2011). By applying the mixed 
trophic impact (MTI) analysis in Ecopath, we assessed the effect of the different ﬁshing ﬂeets 
and gear types on the marine community and the interactions among the species and the 
identiﬁed keystone species (Libralato et al. 2006) in both system states (i.e., 1993 and 2013). 
The assessment of keystone species can be crucial for ecosystem management, as resource 
conservation plans often tend to focus more on species of high commercial value than on 
species of high ecological value (Navia et al. 2016). 
In addition, we used Ecopath software to calculate the economic proﬁt of the ﬁsheries during 
both periods. Information regarding the resource market prices and the costs of ﬁshing (variable 
and ﬁxed costs) for each of the ﬁshing ﬂeets was included in the Ecopath models. 
 
2.2 Methods 
2.2.1 General description of the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem  
The Gulf of Nicoya (10°N, 85°W) is a tidally inﬂuenced estuary along the northwest Paciﬁc 
coast of Costa Rica (Figure 2. 1; Wolff et al. 1998). It is one of the world’s most productive 
tropical estuaries, and its main port, Puntarenas, is the country’s principal ﬁshing ground for 
the artisanal ﬁshing ﬂeet (J. A. Vargas 1995). The model area stretches  from the mouth of the 
Tempisque River in the north to the entrance of the Paciﬁc Ocean in the south and covers  an 
area of 1,530 km 2. According to its shape and bathymetry, the Gulf of Nicoya can be divided 
into the following three regions (Figure 2.1): inner gulf (zone 201), intermediate gulf (zone 
202), and outer gulf (zone 203; Vargas 2016). 
17 
 
Figure 2.1 - Maps of the study area (Gulf of Nicoya, Costa Rica) including the three fishing zones (i.e., inner gulf, 
zone 201; intermediate gulf, zone 202; and outer gulf, zone 203) . 
The shallow interior zone (depth < 10 m) is fringed by mangroves and mud ﬂats and is 
dominated by the seasonal freshwater inﬂow from the Tempisque River (Proyecto Golfos 
2012b). The inner gulf is considered an area of high priority by the Instituto Costarricense de 
Pesca y Acuicultura (INCOPESCA) due to its important function as a reproduction and nursery 
area for commercially important ﬁsh and shrimp species (Alpízar, 2011; Proyecto Golfos, 
2012b; Vargas, 2016). The intermediate zone of the gulf is mostly surrounded by sandy beaches 
and mangroves. The physical parameters of the water masses are subject to intense mixing 
processes due to the interaction between the oceanic water and freshwater from both adjacent 
zones (Proyecto Golfos 2012). The region comprises reproduction areas for small pelagic ﬁsh 
species (Clupeiformes: Clupeidae and Engraulidae), shrimps Litopenaeus spp. (Decapoda: 
Penaeidae), snappers Lutjanus spp. (Perciformes: Lutjanidae), and drums Cynoscion spp. 
(Perciformes: Sciaenidae) and serves as feeding grounds for several commercially targeted 
species, such as the Mexican Barracuda Sphyraena ensis (Perciformes: Sphyraena). The outer 
oceanic zone extends from a minimum depth of 20 m out to 200 m at the shelf edge, is 
surrounded by sandy beaches and rocky shores (Wolff et al. 1998), and is mainly inﬂuenced by 
oceanic waters and the input from the Tárcoles River. The eastern region of the outer gulf is a 
hatching and reproduction site for penaeid shrimps, sharks, and several other ﬁsh species 
(Proyecto Golfos, 2012a; Vargas, 2016) . Similar to other tropical coastal estuarine areas, 
mangroves contribute to primary production in the Gulf of Nicoya by providing organic matter 
and playing an important role in enhancing ﬁsh and invertebrate productivity; thus, mangroves 
strongly support the local ﬁsheries (Hutchison et al. 2014; Wehrtmann and Cortés 2008; Wolff 
2006). 
2.2.2 Model Construction 
2.2.2.1 Modeling approach 
The previous trophic model (GN0) that was initially developed by Wolff et al. (1998) was based 
on data sampled by the RV Victor Hensen Costa Rica expedition (1993–1994) and ﬁsheries 
landings data (INCOPESCA, unpublished data). The GN0 serves as a baseline for this study and 
represents the ecosystem state in 1993–1994. Based on new information obtained from 
collaborative studies, the literature, and the most recent ﬁsheries data, a second model (GN+20) 

18 
was constructed to represent the state of the ecosystem in 2013. To ensure comparability, both 
models comprised the same functional groups. For the modeling task, the open-source software 
Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) version 6.4.4 (Christensen and Walters 2004a) was used. 
Ecopath with Ecosim is based on a set of linear equations for each functional group in the 
system through which the group’s biomass production is connected to the other model groups 
via a prey–predator matrix. For each compartment (i), the basic equation is as follows:  
𝑃௜  =  𝑌௜ +  𝐵௜𝑀2௜ + 𝐸௜ +  𝐵𝐴௜ + 𝑃௜(1 –  𝐸𝐸) 
where Pi is the total production rate of i; Yi is the total  ﬁshery catch rate; M2i is the instantaneous 
predation rate of group i; Bi is the biomass; Ei is the net migration rate (emigration–
immigration); BAi is the biomass accumulation rate; Pi(1 - EE) represents the “other mortality” 
rate of i; and EE is the ecotrophic efﬁciency of i and the proportion of the production that is 
utilized in the system (Christensen and Walters 2004a; Christensen et al. 2005). 
Although equation (1) allows for the balancing of the biomass production in each group with 
its use in the system (and the ﬁshery), a second equation allows for estimating the energy budget 
of each group i as follows: 
Consumptioni = Productioni + Respirationi + Egestion of unassimilated foodi 
The model was built around the main groups targeted by the fisheries. Species with similar 
sizes, diets, consumption, mortality, production rates, and habitats were grouped. The model 
comprised a total of 22 functional groups, including 3 primary producer groups and 10 different 
fish groups. The functional groups and the scientific names of all species that are included in 
the GN models are presented in the Appendix (Table A.1). 
2.2.2.2 Primary producers and zooplankton 
The mangrove coverage along the Costa Rican Pacific coast was estimated to be 413 km 2 
(Wehrtmann and Cortés 2008). In the early 1990s, the mangrove coverage surrounding the Gulf 
of Nicoya was estimated to be approximately 15.2 km2 (Brugnoli-Olivera and Morales-Ramírez 
2008). Since then, the mangrove coverage of the Gulf of Nicoya has declined by 10%, which 
was attributed to land use changes, such as the development of shrimp aquaculture (R. V. Li, 
Barquero, and Fernández 2013). The phytoplankton community is dominated by diatoms and 
flagellates, and the primary productivity of plankton is known to be exceptionally high 
(Brugnoli-Olivera and Morales-Ramírez 2008). Recent studies conducted by researchers from 
the University of Cadiz, Spain, provided information regarding the phytoplankton communities 
at different stations in the Gulf of Nicoya (Requejo 2011). Phytoplankton chlorophyll- a (Chl-
a) concentrations of 1– 16 mg/m 3 were estimated (BIOMARCC 2013a, 2013b). For the 
phytoplankton biomass calculations, the following ratios were used: Chl- a to carbon (g) = 
1:100, predominantly for diatoms; carbon to dry weight organic matter  (g) = 1:2.5; and dry 
organic matter to wet weight organic matter (g) = 1:5 (Parsons et al. 2013; Sathyendranath et 
al. 2009). Regional and seasonal differences were considered; thus, a minimum biomass of 15 
g/m2 was measured in the inner and intermediate gulf areas, which was assumed to be most 
appropriate for the whole gulf area. According to previous studies, the phytoplankton primary 
productivity in the Gulf of Nicoya averages between 0.12 and 2.76 g C·m-2·d-1, and the annual 
gross primary productivity measured in Punta Morales was 450 g C ·m-2·year-1 (Córdoba-
19 
Muñoz 1998; Gocke et al. 2002). The phytoplankton primary productivity was assumed to be 
lower in other zones of the gulf according to previous samples (Wolff et al. 1998). After the 
RV Victor Hensen Costa Rica expedition in the early 1990s, the zooplankton composition was 
once more intensively studied in Punta Morales during 1997  (Brugnoli-Olivera et al. 2004) ; 
the dominance of copepods in the zooplankton community was confirmed, and large seasonal 
variations in the biomass were detected. An average annual zooplankton biomass of 4 g/m 2 of 
water column was assumed for both models based on the work of Brugnoli-Olivera et al. (2004). 
2.2.2.3 Fishing fleets and target resources 
In Costa Rican Pacific waters, the tuna fleet and the large pelagic longline fleet are the most 
important in terms of catch volume (FAO 2007; Trujillo et al. 2012). However, inside the Gulf 
of Nicoya, the small-scale artisanal fishing sector is of major economic and social importance 
(Lozano and Heinen 2016a; Trujillo et al. 2012). Four artisanal fishing fleets operate in the gulf 
with different types of gear, including gill nets, lines (bottom longlines and drifting longlines), 
handlines, and manual gear (traps and mollusk extraction), and target a variety of demersal and 
coastal species (BIOMARCC-SINAC- GIZ 2013b; 2013a) . Gill nets are prominently used, 
mostly with small mesh sizes (< 7.62 cm [< 3 in]), which are illegal in the Gulf of Nicoya. 
Catches are diverse but dominated by drums (Perciformes: Sciaenidae), catfishes (Siluriformes: 
Ariidae), snooks (Perciformes: Centropomidae), and different penaeid shrimp species (Alpízar 
2015). Additionally, two semi-industrial fishing fleets operate in the gulf: shrimp trawlers and 
sardine purse-seiners. The purse seine vessels target small pelagic fish species in the 
intermediate and outer zones of the Gulf of Nicoya, while the bottom trawl fleet targets white 
shrimps and several other shrimp species in the outer gulf. In this fishery, the capture of 
nontarget (bycatch) species drastically exceeds the shrimp catches (Figure 2.2). Due to 
shrinking resources in shallow waters, the shrimp trawler fleet has progressively moved toward 
deeper waters outside of the gulf (Wehrtmann and Nielsen-Muñoz 2009) ; thus, the deepwater 
shrimp species that are landed in Puntarenas (kolibri shrimp Solenocera agassizii, bigheaded 
shrimp Heterocarpus vicarious , and three-spined nylon shrimp Heterocarpus affinis ) are 
presumably fished outside of the gulf and were excluded from the model (UNEP 2004). Bycatch 
species caught outside of the gulf are not included in the model, as the Costa Rican deepwater 
shrimp fishery discards almost the entire bycatch, including species of commercial value 
(Wehrtmann and Nielsen-Muñoz 2009). The landings of the six fishing fleets were entered 
separately into the Ecopath model. 
20 
 
Figure 2.2 – Semi-Industrial catch and effort (Gulf of Nicoya, Costa Rica):(A) Catches (metric tons [t]/year) and 
(B) fishing effort (number of days fishing/year) of the semi-industrial fleet in the Gulf of Nicoya during 1994 – 
2014 (in panel A, light gray = shrimp catch; dark gray = sardine catch; black = other fish catch).  
All fish groups are targeted by at least one of the six identified fishing fleets. For each model 
group, the following basic input parameters were gathered: reported artisanal and semi-
industrial catches, biomass ( B), production- to-biomass ratios ( P/B), consumption-to-biomass 
ratios ( Q/B; Table 2.1), and the diet composition of each species or group (Table A.2). 
Information regarding the diet composition was obtained from Wolff et al. (1998) and the recent 
literature (Lopez-Peralta and Arcila 2002; Olson and Watters 2003; Robertson 2009). For 
additional references, please refer to the Appendix (Table A.3). The artisanal landings data from 
each zone of the Gulf of Nicoya were obtained from the Costa Rican monthly national landing 
statistics (INCOPESCA, unpublished data). The provided time series consists of monthly 
records of the main target species and groups (fish and invertebrates) that were fished and 
landed in the Gulf of Nicoya. The landings (kg) were separated by fishing fleet and gear. The 
estimated bycatch of the shrimp trawlers and discard estimates were included. The monthly 
records of the semi-industrial landings and the fishing effort, which was measured as the 
number of days fishing (as well as some data about the number of fishing trips) in the Gulf of 
Nicoya, were provided by INCOPESCA (Departamento de Investigación y Desarrollo). 
Additional yearly sampling data (INCOPESCA, Departamento de Investigación y Desarrollo) 
were obtained to estimate the relative species composition within the target groups. To enter 
the data into the model, the monthly landings data, discards, and shrimp trawl bycatch data were 
converted into metric tons per square kilometer (based on the model area of 1,530 km 2) per 
year. 
2.2.2.4 Calculations of the model parameters 
The biomass estimates in the early model (GN 0) were based on fishery catches and beam and 
otter trawl survey data. Trends in fishery catches can serve as a first indicator of the population 
biomass of the target resources. For the 2013 model (GN +20), the resource biomass values per 
unit area (metric tons/km2) were calculated from the catch data ( Y) and estimated fishing 
mortality (F) by using the following formula proposed by Beverton and Holt (1957): B = Y / F: 
The recent exploitation rates and F-values for most of the fish and shrimp species were obtained 
from INCOPESCA stock assessment studies (Alpízar 2011; 2014; 2015; Alpízar et al. 2013; 

21 
Alpízar and Vásquez 2010; 2012)  and from research conducted by the Universidad Nacional 
Costa Rica (Quiróz Aizpurúa 2014). Stock information on morays/ eels, catfishes, and flatfishes 
is scarce, and the biomass had to be estimated from the catch data and the previous model 
(Wolff et al. 1998). The values were compared to those from other studies conducted in similar 
areas. The biomass values for three functional groups - microphytobenthos, endobenthos, and 
seabirds - were estimated according to information available in the literature regarding their 
abundance and biomass estimations, which are based on the previous model (see Table A.3 for 
detailed references).
