3.4.2 Uncertainty and Sensitivity Analysis 
Although we reported a substantial reduction of the officially reported fishing effort in the gulf, 
it can be assumed that unreported catches, fishing without licenses and fishing during the closed 
season have occurred during the observational period. These unreported fishing activities 
cannot be quantified and are thus not part of the study. 
The Ecopath models have been carefully validated with a prescribed balancing routine of Link 
(2010). The balancing process of the models has been described in Alms and Wolff (2019). 
Wee adhered to the suggestions of best-practice in Ecopath with Ecosim food-web models for 
ecosystem-based management by Heymans et al. (2016). Adding data to the model such as 
model drivers and reference data sets using the Ecosim tools helped to further validate the 
quality and accuracy of the created static Ecopath model. The model was additionally calibrated 
with historical fishing datasets and the comparison of the model predictions to reference data 
allowed for the validation of the model performance (Johanna Jacomina Heymans et al. 2016). 
The calculated EwE Pedigree Index of 0.29, based on the confidence intervals of all input 
parameters, indicated that the model’s level of precision is in the intermediate to the upper 
range, compared to other published Ecopath models worldwide (Morissette 2007). We believe 
that the AICc is a good measure for validation of the selected model. We followed the 
recommendation to adapt the AIC for small sample sizes (Burnham and Anderson 2004). 
The aim was to provide a holistic modeling approach, in the sense looking at combined effects 
influencing the species biomass variations. We covered trophic effects, the effects of fishing, 
the most prominent climate variables, and physical and chemical factors (also as proxy for 
pollution). However, the selection of factors was limited by the availability of time-series data 
and not every single possible parameter could be tested. Some factors, such as human 
disturbances, sedimentation, solid waste pollution and agricultural pollution, could not be 
57 
assessed. For future research it would be useful to incorporate social-ecological interactions 
into the model. 
3.4.3 Management Outlook and Implications 
The present study leads to the conclusion that fisheries management policies entirely based on 
fisheries information only, with no consideration of the influence of environmental factors is 
not sufficient for the development of a holistic management strategy. Both fishing regulations 
and environmental monitoring needs to go hand in hand. We found that the shrimp trawl fleet 
predominately affects the distribution of important target species. Thus, the advised fishing ban 
and the expiration of licenses concerning the trawler can be seen as a promising management 
measure. The sardine purse-seine fleet needs to be seen critically as we could highlight the 
importance of small pelagic fish to the ecosystem and their susceptibility to climate change. 
The impact of the artisanal fleet is minor compared to the semi-industrial fleet. However, the 
gillnet fleet using small mesh sizes can be potentially harmful for sustainable fisheries. To 
reduce illegal fishing activities stronger control mechanisms and rigor enforcement are needed. 
Furthermore, the manual mollusk extraction needs to be limited, as yearly around 8 million 
individuals are harvested from the Gulf of Nicoya (SINAC 2019). 
Environmental monitoring needs to be considered and combined with fisheries assessment 
studies, due to the considerable influence of these factors on the entire trophic system as well 
as their social-economic impacts. A regular monitoring program for the Gulf of Nicoya is 
necessary to track changes in primary production, and phytoplankton community composition 
in the Gulf of Nicoya, that may be driven by both increased coastal pollution and environmental 
change. To apply appropriate management measures, a forecasting approach is needed (Clark 
et al. 2001) that takes into account knowledge of the current and predicted ecosystem structure 
and functioning (Valette-Silver and Scavia 2003). 
Our study may be used as a reference to further assess the regional impacts of future climate 
variations and extreme events in an ecosystem already altered by the fisheries. In the Gulf of 
Nicoya, like in other tropical environments, we need to consider the effects of increasing 
temperatures, as stated by Cheung et al. (2013) and Palomares and Pauly (2019). In contrast to 
colder regions, no species adapted for warm regions will be available to replace species that are 
displaced by the high temperature thus, there will most likely be a temperature-driven loss of 
species in these areas. 
Of particular relevance for future management scenarios are regional changes that have been 
predicted by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) until the year 2100. 
According to the IPCC the Gross Primary Productivity is expected to decrease, mainly due to 
an increase in Colored Dissolved Organic Matter (CDOM), which absorbs a fraction of the 
Photosynthetically Active Radiation (PAR) (Thrane et al. 2014; Wolf and Heuschele 2018) . 
One can thus expect that Chl a concentrations decrease in the Gulf of Nicoya and that 
phytoplankton composition, quantity and distribution will change. In addition, mangrove 
coverage is expected to further decrease due to sea level rise and changes in salinity (Ward et 
al., 2016). While mangroves only account for an estimate of 1% of the gulf ’s primary 
production (Wolff et al. 1998), they contribute to the system’s productivity in the Gulf of 
Nicoya by providing organic matter and protected areas, enhancing fish and invertebrate 
58 
productivity (Wolff et al. 1998). Considering the predicted decrease in primary productivity, it 
seems necessary for a sustainable ecosystem management to adapt the volume of fishery 
catches to potentially lower overall resource productivity. 
Furthermore, there is a high uncertainty concerning the future dynamics of the ENSO cycle. 
The frequency and intensity of El Niño events is likely to be altered by climate change, however 
it is unknown to what extent (e.g. Cai et al. 2014; Collins et al. 2010) . As our simulations 
suggest, catch and biomass fluctuations in the Gulf of Nicoya are linked to ENSO related 
variations in the Equatorial Pacific Sea Level Pressure, and a constant monitoring of the 
regional ENSO impact is thus imperative. 
As we have suggested previously (Alms and Wolff, 2019), it is crucial to think of measures to 
sustain primary productivity and to protect keystone groups, benthos and shrimps in the Gulf 
of Nicoya, but also adequately manage the mid-trophic level species such as small pelagic and 
small demersal fish. It is known that in the gulf, like in other coastal areas and estuaries with 
high abiotic variability (Heymans et al. 2014), these groups are central for ecosystem 
functioning and need to be prioritized in future management plans. In addition to adequate 
fisheries regulation, possible measures for improving the overall management of the Gulf of 
Nicoya could be the improvement of wastewater management and treatment and solid waste 
disposal plans. For the conservation of the coastal marine environment the further protection 
and restoration of mangroves is an important issue that needs to be further pursued (SINAC 
2019). 
Furthermore, our models can be used to directly engage stakeholders in the management plans, 
as it has been attempted by Sánchez-Jiménez et al. (2019). The EwE software was used as an 
interactive communication tool with artisanal fishers in the Gulf of Nicoya, to facilitate 
discussions on changes in the fishing ecosystem over time.

IN GULF OF NICOYA USING ECOSYSTEM MODELLING

Chapter 4 
 
Exploration of fisheries management policies in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) using 
ecosystem modelling 
Viola Alms, Giovanni Romagnoni, and Matthias Wolff 
 
This is the author´s version of the work. Please cite the final version as:  
Alms, V., Romagnoni, G., and Wolff, M. (2022). Exploration of fisheries management policies 
in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) using ecosystem modelling. Ocean & Coastal Management, 
230, 106349. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ocecoaman.2022.106349 
https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ocecoaman.2022.106349

Contribution statement 
Project idea:      Viola Alms 
Design of the work:     Viola Alms 
Acquisition or interpretation of data:  Viola Alms supported by Giovanni Romagnoni 
Drafted manuscript revised by Giovanni Romagnoni and Matthias Wolff. 
 
  
61 
Abstract 
The Gulf of Nicoya is a highly productive estuary located at the Pacific coast of Costa Rica. 
Previous studies have used trophic models to examine changes in the biomass of key species 
and in the food web in the last 25 years, revealing an overfished, degraded system with 
decreasing biomass of valuable target species. The ecosystem degradation was mainly driven 
by intensive fishing, while climate variations affected resource productivity additionally. This 
study tested the effectiveness of alternative scenarios including combinations of the current top-
down fishing policy and the ban on shrimp trawling, together with a participatory management 
scenario developed in a previously performed stakeholder workshop. In parallel, the automated 
fishing policy search of the Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) software was used to explore an 
optimized alternative management scenario. The analysis indicates that the ban on trawling is 
an important measure to allow for the recovery of certain target species, such as shrimps, 
demersal fish and their predators. However, this ban would not suffice to substantially rebuild 
the biomass of all key species in the system. Thus, two possible alternative management 
scenarios are proposed: in the first one, the economic losses are minimized, ecosystem health 
increases by 10% (by rebuilding target species biomass) and employment provided by fishing 
decreases (-15%). In the second scenario, higher economic losses are accepted (mainly for the 
semi-industrial fisheries sector) which allows for a higher increase in ecosystem health and 
biodiversity. Both scenarios call for additional reductions in fishing efforts, mainly by the semi-
industrial purse-seine fleet and the artisanal longline fleet. This study exemplifies how holistic 
ecosystem models can be used for management advice, future policymaking and how 
stakeholders can be engaged in this process. 
Keywords: Trophic model, fishing policy, fisheries management, Ecopath with Ecosim, 
participatory management 
  
62 
4.1 Introduction 
The marine fishing sector in Costa Rica has steadily grown in the last decades, advancing its 
gear and boats, increasing the fishing range and its catch effectivity. These development lead 
to overexploitation and also increased conflicts among fishing sectors due to decreasing 
resources (Lozano and Heinen 2016b). Especially the small-scale fisheries is the primary source 
of income and food for many coastal communities, and at the same time the most vulnerable to 
overexploitation (Carrillo et al. 2019). The situation calls for an effective management policy 
and suitable marine conservation strategies.  
Costa Rica has already proven to understand the urgency of protecting its environment. The 
country aims to be among the global leaders in sustainability and has successfully established 
a functioning environmental protection system, fights climate change with an ambitious 
decarbonization plan (UNEP, 2019). One quarter of the terrestrial area is under some kind of 
protection and Costa Rica has managed to successfully promote reforestation. Although Costa 
Rica pushed for ocean conservation, in the past it has played a subordinate role in this process, 
as less than 1% of its marine area is protected. This is especially striking as the potential of 
marine conservation is immense, with Costa Rica having an Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ), 
ten times larger than its terrestrial area. Now more than ever before significant attention is paid 
to marine conservation. It is not without reason that the United Nations proclaimed a Decade 
of Ocean Science for Sustainable Development (2021-2030). The Ocean Decade is linked to 
the 2030 Agenda and facilitates a variety of marine conservation strategies, like the goal to 
achieve a healthy, resilient and sustainably harvested ocean (Johanna J Heymans et al. 2020; 
Ryabinin et al. 2019). In this context, a promising example of an international cooperation to 
promote the conservation and sustainable use of marine resources is the Eastern Tropical Pacific 
Marine Corridor (Corredor Mariano del Pacífico Este Tropical - CMAR 2019). This large 
Marine Protected Area (MPA) is supposed to protect marine biodiversity with special attention 
on migratory species, for an area across the EEZ boundaries of Ecuador, Costa Rica, Colombia 
and Panama (Enright et al. 2021), which makes it a significant large-scale initiative.  
Also, on the regional scale, some measures have been taken in the past to protect the Gulf of 
Nicoya ecosystem, Costa Rica’s main fishing ground. The goals of these policy measures were 
to ensure a more sustainable use of harvested resources and to protect the existence of the small-
scale fisheries, that is traditionally rooted in the coastal communities around the estuary and is 
of high economic and social importance. 
The measures of input control include fishing effort limitation through granting licenses and a 
seasonal closure (in Spanish: “veda”) to all fishing fleets, as well as the technical limitation of 
gear size (mesh sizes > 6.35 cm [2.5 in]). However, most of these top-down enforced 
management rules failed either due to noncompliance of fishers or the lack of control capacities. 
Furthermore, Costa Rica is supporting Marine Areas of Responsible Fishing (AMPRs), areas 
of exclusive harvesting for small-scale fishing communities fostering co-management. 
However, to turn the tide the fishing regulations had to be supplemented with a more drastical 
enforcement, the ban on shrimp trawling for the entire Gulf of Nicoya. In 2013 semi-industrial 
shrimp trawling was declared unconstitutional in Costa Rica and no further licenses were issued 
since 2015. The last remaining shrimp trawling license in the Gulf of Nicoya expired in 2019. 
63 
This was a major step towards a more sustainable fishery and a reduction in fishing effort. Not 
only shrimp species benefit from the ban but also a variety of bycatch species, as well as the 
bottom flora and fauna.  
To evaluate the effectiveness of these efforts, the first research question of the present study is 
to assess whether the current ban on trawling is the optimal management solution and whether 
it is sufficient to achieve a recovery of a variety of species and the restoration of the of the Gulf 
of Nicoya marine ecosystem.  
The assessment of the ecosystem benefits, was based on  the principles of the Ecosystem 
Approach to Fisheries (EAF) (FAO 2002). The EAF considers the trophic interactions among 
functional groups within the ecosystem, and includes environmental, ecological, and 
anthropogenic impacts on an ecosystem in the management consideration. Following the EAF 
approach, one can find optimal sustainable rates of exploitation under the condition of 
biodiversity conservation, increase in ecosystem health and/or rebuilding stocks of target 
species (Forrest et al. 2015). It provides clear benefits compared to single-species management 
approaches, that are often insufficient (Cochrane 2002; FAO 2012). In the context of the EAF 
approach, we used a holistic ecosystem model of the Gulf of Nicoya (Alms and Wolff 2019), 
developed with the open-source software Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE). Ecosim simulations 
were used to depict effects of changes in the fishing effort levels on the species/group biomasses 
and ecosystem health over time.  
In the first step to assess the effectivity of the different management options, we tested the 
success of the policy that is currently in place (ban of trawling), which was afterwards compared 
to other hypothetical management plans, using a scenario-based approach. For this approach 
we developed a set of hypothecial scenarios. One of these scenarios is a stakeholder-based 
policy based on the reduction of gillnet fleet efforts by 25% aimed at allowing high trophic 
level species to rebuild their stock biomass. It has been suggested by the fishermen themselves 
during participatory management workshops based on the visualization and information about 
the ecosystem health and future perspectives using the EwE modelling software (Sánchez 
Jiménez et al. 2021). The stakeholder-based policy was especially developed to foster 
participatory management in the gulf of Nicoya. It is known, that besides finding the optimal 
balance between different management goals for successful policymaking, it is important to 
consider the interests and knowledge of local stakeholders, thereby increasing the compliance 
of all actors (Wilson et al. 2006). In fact, according to the UN Sustainable Development Goals 
(SDGs), fisheries play a pivotal role in food security but also on job provision for coastal 
communities (Food security and decent work conditions; SDGs 2 and 8). Especially tropical 
artisanal coastal fisheries face a variety of challenges that threaten future sustainability and at 
the same time, the management of artisanal fisheries is particularly difficult (Batista et al. 2014). 
As a further scenario, the combination of the artisanal gillnet effort together with the ban of 
shrimp trawling was selected. The scenario deliberately affects both the artisanal and the semi-
industrial sector, to consider the conflicts in the use of coastal resources as well as competition 
between artisanal and semi-industrial fishing.  
Our second research question was to assess, whether an alternative fishing policy can be found 
that is more effective than the current policy or the stakeholder-based policy. Thus, in the 
second step, we used an automated modelling routine to explore other random management 
64 
scenarios. For the analysis, the fishing policy optimization routine embedded in the EwE 
software was applied. This routine maximizes defined management goals based on weighted 
policy objectives. This approach allows for the definition of specific management objectives 
that are realistic trade-offs between ecosystem and social-economic benefits. Ecosystem 
benefits include ecosystem health, a balanced biodiversity and stable biomass levels of the 
functional group in the system, whereas social and economic benefits include the employment 
through fishing and related processes, as well as fishing itself as a source of income (FAO 
2020). The routine is a powerful tool to find a successful fishing policy, that should at best 
entail benefits for sustainability, society, equity of costs and benefits, and decrease of conflicts 
e.g., from competition between artisanal and semi-industrial flee ts (FAO 2020; Hilborn and 
Walters 2013).  
The method allows for the calculation of profit from fishing for each fishing fleet in a given 
scenario. It is evident that all measures taken towards a healthier ecosystem and the recovery 
of species, will entail short-term economic losses and social constraints in the Gulf of Nicoya, 
e.g. Ovando et al. (2016). However, there will also be consequences if no actions are taken, due 
to the further depletion of valuable resources by continued extensive fishing. This has been 
observable by the decrease of valuable shrimps as well as by increasing catches of low value 
(and low-trophic-level) species, that have already caused a tremendous economic loss of 50% 
of the profit (Alms and Wolff 2019).  
Our study is an example of how holistic ecosystem models can be used for policymaking and 
how the models can be useful for participatory management, to facilitate the cooperation 
between stakeholders and to increase compliance. 
 
4.2 Materials and Methods 
4.2.1 Study area and fishing fleets 
The Gulf of Nicoya (10°N, 85°W) is a tropical estuary at the northwest Paciﬁc coast of Costa 
Rica (Figure 4.1). The model area stretches from the northern inland (mouth of the Tempisque 
River) to the entrance of the Paciﬁc Ocean in the south and covers an area of 1,530 km². The 
inner part of the Gulf is characterized by shallow waters and tidally influenced mud- and 
sandflats. The outer oceanic zone extends to a maximum depth of 200 m at the shelf edge (Wolff 
et al.1998). The Gulf of Nicoya has an exceptionally high productivity, which makes it Costa 
Rica’s principal ﬁshing ground for the artisanal ﬁshing ﬂeet, which provides around 55% of the 
total catch in the Gulf of Nicoya (in 2013). The small-scale artisanal fishing sector is of major 
economic and social importance for approximately 2000 fishermen and more than 20 coastal 
communities (Lozano and Heinen 2016b; Trujillo et al. 2012). 
65 
 
Figure 4.1 - Maps of the study area (Gulf of Nicoya, Costa Rica) , from Alms and Wolff (2020). 
Four artisanal fishing fleets operate in the gulf with different boat sizes and gear types that 
target a variety of demersal and coastal species (BIOMARCC-SINAC-GIZ 2013a). Gillnets are 
predominantly used, followed by the longline, handline and manual mollusk extraction, which 
is mostly carried out by women from the coastal communities, in combination with the manual 
trap fisheries targeting different crustaceans.  
The main port of the Gulf of Nicoya, Puntarenas, is also the shrimp trawlers landing site for the 
semi-industrial fishing fleets - the purse-seine fleet and the shrimp trawl fleet. The purse-seine 
vessels target small pelagic fish species ( Anchoa spp., Cetengraulis mysticetus and 
Opisthonema spp.) in the intermediate and outer zones of the Gulf of Nicoya. The bottom trawl 
fleet used to target several shrimp species, mostly in the outer gulf, however the capture of 
bycatch species drastically exceeded the shrimp catches (Clarke et al. 2016). As a result, shrimp 
trawling has been banned from the Gulf of Nicoya since 2019. For further descriptions of the 
ecosystem please refer to (Alms and Wolff 2019; 2020). 
4.2.2 Model description 
For our study we used the balanced trophic model of the Gulf of Nicoya (Table 4.1), constructed 
by Alms and Wolff (2019) based on collaborative studies and the most recent fisheries data 
available (INCOPESCA, unpublished data). It represents the state of the ecosystem in the year 
2013. The model comprised a total of 22 functional groups, including 3 primary producer 
groups and 10 different ﬁsh groups and was balanced and fitted to time-series data (historic 
fisheries catches and effort data). 
For the modelling task, the open-source software Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) version 6.4.4 
(Christensen and Walters 2004) was used. Ecopath with Ecosim is based on a set of linear 
equations for each functional group in the system through which the group’s biomass 
production is connected to other model groups via a prey–predator matrix. For each 
compartment (i), the basic equation is as follows (1): 
𝑃௜ = 𝑌௜ + 𝐵௜𝑀2௜ + 𝐸௜ + 𝐵𝐴௜ + 𝑃௜(1 − 𝐸𝐸), 
 

66 
where Pi is the total production rate of i; Yi is the total fishery catch rate; M2i is the instantaneous 
predation rate of group i; B i is the biomass; Ei is the net migration rate (emigration–
immigration); BAi is the biomass accumulation rate; P i(1-EE) represents the “other mortality” 
rate of i; and EE is the ecotrophic efficiency of i and the proportion of the production that is 
utilized in the system (Christensen et al. 2005; Christensen and Walters 2004a) . Although 
equation (1) allows balancing of the biomass production in each group with its use in the system 
(and the fishery), with a second equation estimating the energy budget of each group i is 
possible as follows (2):  
𝐶𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑢𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛௜ = 𝑃𝑟𝑜𝑑𝑢𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛௜ + 𝑅𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑖𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛௜ + 𝐸𝑔𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 𝑜𝑓 𝑢𝑛𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑓𝑜𝑜𝑑௜ 
The model was built around main groups targeted by the fisheries. Species with similar sizes, 
diets, consumption, mortality, production rates, and habitats were grouped.

The model has previously been balanced based on the suggestions provided by Link (2010), as 
described in Alms and Wolff (2019). The EwE modelling routines are particularly sensitive to 
the selected vulnerability settings; thus, we ran the analysis with the optimized model fit to 
time-series data. We used the Stepwise Fitting Procedure for automated fitting of Ecopath with 
Ecosim models (Scott et al. 2016) as described in Alms and Wolff (2020). Compared to the 
68 
model parameterization of Alms and Wolff (2019), successive reanalysis suggested some minor 
changes. In particular, due to the high predation rates of the abundant small demersal fish 
species, we opted to slightly reduce the vulnerability of shrimps to the predation of small 
demersal (from 3 to 1). This modification allowed more realistic model behavior while 
maintaining the model performance (i.e., the sum of squares representing the fit to time series 
data did not change substantially).  
4.2.3 Management scenarios and simulations 
To find the optimal management policy for the Gulf of Nicoya, we used a scenario-based 
approach. We tested the losses and benefits of the social and economic value (profit, number 
of jobs) and of the conservational criteria (ecosystem health, biodiversity) caused by each of 
the different management scenarios (Figure 4.2), based on the fishing effort control of each 
fishing fleet. We also tested scenarios that would result in a future increase or decrease in the 
total biomass of the ecosystem, as well as for the biomass of each functional group after 20 
years of a given management scenario, using the EwE simulation tool. We aimed for a medium-
term strategy, according to the suggestion of the FAO management cycle or EAF (Bianchi 
2008); thus, we projected the management scenario 10 years into the future.  
We assessed the effectiveness of the current management plan, which successively banned 
shrimp trawlers from the Gulf of Nicoya until 2019 by not renewing the corresponding fishing 
licenses. (scenario 1, Figure 4.2) Furthermore, the benefits of a 25% effort reduction of the 
artisanal gill net fleet were evaluated, based on suggestions from a collaborative stakeholder 
workshop in Puntarenas, Costa Rica (Sánchez-Jiménez et al. 2019), that included the perception 
of the fishermen themselves. Additionally, a combination of the existing ban on trawling and 
the gillnet effort reduction was considered (scenarios 2 and 3, respectively Figure 4.2). 
 
69 
 
Figure 4.2 - Schematic overview of the assessed management scenarios for the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica). 
Furthermore, the Fishing Policy Search tool of the EwE package was used to explore alternative 
management scenarios. The automated search routine allows the assessment of appropriate 
management scenarios while considering different management objectives or a combination of 
them (Christensen and Walters 2004b). The output can be optimized for socio-economic goals 
and ecosystem conservation goals.  
The fishing policy search routine uses a nonlinear optimization procedure known as the 
Davidson-Fletcher-Powell (DFP) method, repetitively changing the relative fishing rates in 
multiple model runs, to improve an objective function (Walters et al. 2002). Subsequently, the 
EwE simulations were used to analyze the given output (effort rates) of the search routine. The 
annual economic discount rate was kept at 4% for all simulations. The rate describes the costs 
and benefits that arise at different times and implicates at which rate society is willing to trade 
off present for future benefits (NOAA 1999). It can be distinguished between a project with 
long-term benefits versus a project with short-term benefits and long-term costs. 
For the single-objective optimization runs the model optimizes the fishing effort of the fishing 
fleets in order to maximize only one of the management goals (Table 4.2), for which a weight 
of 1 (W=1) was assigned, whereas the other objectives were assigned with a weight of 0 (W=0). 
In each run, one of the objectives was maximized and the effects on all other objectives were 
studied (Table 4.2).  
In the next step, we assessed multi-objective optimizations, a more realistic approach to include 
the interests of different stakeholders, by testing a combination of several management 
objectives. The goal was to identify a trade-off, which balances ecosystem conservation goals, 
social value (employment provided by fishing) and profits from fishing, and is not detrimental 

70 
for one single sector.  For the following analysis, a combination of different weights was 
assigned to different management objectives (Table 4.2). Following the approach suggested by 
Christensen and Walters (2004b), we modified the weight of each objective until we found a 
compromise that considers all objectives in a suitable manner. We selected two compromise-
scenarios for further analysis. Firstly, we present Compromise A, which implies less extreme 
effort reductions for one single fleet, affecting both the artisanal and semi-industrial fishing 
sector. Secondly, Compromise B was selected, a trade-off oriented towards ecosystem health 
and increasing biodiversity, with severe effort reductions for the semi-industrial fleets. The 
fishing effort of the selected scenarios was applied to the EwE model to simulate future changes 
in the ecosystem. The policy search considers the following management goals. 
Table 4.2  – The Fishing Policy Search routine : Relative weights for the single-objective and multi-objective 
optimization runs of the policy search routine (EwE) for the assessed management scenarios for the Gulf of Nicoya. 
Single objective: One objective is maximized at a time, assigned a value of 1 (all others assigned a value of 0). 
Multi-objective: All goals that are considered for maximization are assigned a value>1.

4.2.3.1 The economic value (profit from fishing) and social value (employment) 
The selling prices at the landing site were entered into the model as off-vessel prices (US$ per 
kg) for each functional group and were used to calculate the revenue from fishing 
(INCOPESCA, unpublished market price statistics). The costs of each fishing fleet were entered 
as a percentage of the fishery’s total value in the given year (Table 4.3). We distinguished 
between fixed costs, which accrue without any fishing activity taking place; and variable costs 
(e.g., fuel and ice) which are directly related to the fishing effort of the fleet. The relative 
percentages of fixed costs and effort-related costs were entered for each of the artisanal and 
semi-industrial fleets in the Gulf of Nicoya separately. These values are based on an economic 
study of the Costa Rican fishing fleet by Coyle et al., (2015), who calculated the expenses and 
profits per kg catch in the Gulf of Nicoya. Additionally, economic information provided by the 
project “Biodiversidad Marino Costera en Costa Rica, Desarrollo de Capacidades y Adaptación 
al Cambio Climático” were used (BIOMARCC-SINAC-GIZ 2013b) as well as information on 
the value chain of the fisheries in the Gulf of Nicoya (Proyecto Golfos 2012b). These values 
were then compared to other similar studies (Lercari and Arreguín-Sánchez 2008; Gasalla et al. 
2010). The profit is defined as the value of the catch (catch price, by species) - the cost of fishing 
(fixed + variable costs) or, more formally, 
71 
𝜋௙ = ෍ 𝑝௞ ,௙ ∗ 𝐻௞ ,௙
௄
௞
− ൫𝐹𝐶௙ + 𝐸௙ ∗ 𝑉𝐶௙ ൯ 
Where 𝜋௙  is the profit for fleet f, determined as the sum of harvest  H for all species  k in the 
portfolio of caught species K in fleet f, multiplied by the fleet- and species- specific price  𝑝௞ ,௙  
minus the fixed cost FC and variable cost VC multiplied by effort E. The social value for each 
fishing fleet was calculated as the fleet specific number of jobs per catch value. The social 
employment weights are displayed in Table 4.3.

4.2.3.2 Ecosystem conservation 
The first of the three ecosystem conservation goals (4c) is the mandated rebuilding of species 
biomass, which allows for the assessment of the policy measures needed for preserving or 
rebuilding the population of certain functional groups in the ecosystem. A threshold biomass 
(relative to the biomass in Ecopath) was entered for each functional group, and the policy 
routine optimizes the fleet effort structure to most effectively reach these biomass levels for as 
many functional groups as possible. As threshold biomasses we chose to use the baseline 
biomass values of the ecosystem state in 1993 (Alms and Wolff 2019). These values originate 
from our baseline model of the Gulf of Nicoya representing the ecosystem state in 1993. Due 
to our modelling results, we believe that at that time the ecosystem was in a healthier state, with 
higher biomass levels across the ecosystem, and a higher biodiversity compared to 2013 after 
two decades of further degradation. As ecological weighting factors for the conservation of 
each functional group (structured relative weight) we used the reciprocal P/B ratios, which put 
a higher weight on long-lived, slow-growing groups. This approach allows for the maximization 
of the trophic transfer efficiency, increasing biodiversity and the maintenance of ecosystem 
stability (Okey and Wright 2004; Walters et al. 2002) (Table 4.4). 
  
72 
Table 4.4 -Mandated rebuilding of species biomass: (scenario 4c) threshold biomasses defined as desired level of 
restoration for each functional group. We chose the biomass values of the Gulf of Nicoya baseline model from 
1993 (Alms and Wolff 2019), representing a healthier state of the ecosystem. The structured relative weight was 
calculated as the reciprocal P/B ratios of each functional group, representing the relative importance of each 
group for the system.

The second ecosystem conservation objective is the ecosystem structure (health). To classify 
the ecosystem health of the model, different network parameters can be used, such as the 
maturity of the ecosystem, number of trophic flows and age structure, average trophic level of 
the catch.  
In order to maximize ecosystem health, the automated policy search uses an ecosystem maturity 
index (Odum 1969), calculating the longevity-weighted summed biomass over all functional 
groups (Christensen and Walters 2004b). The group-specific biomass/production ratio indicates 
the longevity of functional groups and can thus be used as a proxy for ecosystem health (W. W. 
L. Cheung and Sumaila 2008).  
The third objective is the ecosystem biodiversity. As a measure of biodiversity, a modified 
version of Kemptonʼs Q75 index (Kempton 2002) is used to calculate an index of biomass 
diversity (Christensen and Walters 2004b). 
The goal is to achieve increasing diversity, especially in higher trophic levels (Cury et al. 2011; 
Geers et al. 2016). Often, increasing biodiversity is accompanied by increasing ecosystem 
maturity and a healthy ecosystem (Cury et al. 2011). 
73 
4.3 Results 
4.3.1 Ecosim simulations (Scenarios 1 – 3) 
4.3.1.1 Scenario 1: Existing ban on trawling from the entire Gulf of Nicoya. 
Model simulations of the future effects of the existing ban on shrimp trawling indicate that it 
will most likely lead to the desired long-term recovery of shrimp biomass, with a projected 
increase of 6,5% (until 2033). The policy measure also entails increasing biomass of demersal 
fish species and related predatory species (Figure 4.3), as well as an overall increase in the total 
ecosystem biomass (Figure 4.4). However, it might not be sufficient to substantially rebuild the 
biomass of all key species in the ecosystem (Figure 4.3). The elimination of the shrimp trawling 
industry from the Gulf of Nicoya leads to a substantial loss of profit for the fisheries. The value 
of the catch, landed by the semi-industrial fleet decreases by around 85% (Figure 4.5). 
However, it can be observed that artisanal catches could increase slightly (gillnet, handline, 
longline fleet) with the ban on trawling (Figure 4.5). 
 
Figure 4.3 - Relative difference in the biomass of each functional group in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) after 
20 years of a selected management scenario compared to the baseline year. 

74 
 
Figure 4.4 - The expected relative increase [%] in the total biomass of all functional groups   
 (sum of biomass) in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica), presented for each of the selected management scenarios 
after 20 years (2033) compared to the baseline year (2013). 
 

75 
 
Figure 4.5 – Relative differences in the catch, fishing effort and profit: Relative difference in the catch (A) and 
fishing effort (B) of each fishing fleet compared to the baseline value (dashed line) and relative difference in the 
profit of the artisanal and semi-industrial fishing sector (C) in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) after 20 years of a 
selected management scenario compared to the baseline model without any policy restrictions. 
4.3.1.2 Scenario 2: Effort reduction of the gillnet fleet by 25% 
The stakeholder-proposed effort reduction for the gillnet fleet (-25%) implies benefits for 
several species, such as flatfish, small demersal fish as well as mackerel and barracuda (Figure 
4.3). However, the benefits for most species are only minor and the overall ecosystem biomass 
cannot be increased as effectively, compared to the other management scenarios. At the same 
time, the effort reduction causes a significant loss of profit (more than 20%) and jobs to the 
artisanal fishing sector (Figure 4.5a). 
4.3.1.3 Scenario 3: Combination of scenarios 1 and 2 
A combined approach, that includes adding the gillnet effort reduction to the existing ban of 
trawling is a powerful measure, causing an apparent increase in the biomass of several target 
species. The biomass of shrimp, small demersals, catfish and top predators can be sustainably 
increased (up to 25%) over the long term (Figure 4.3), and the ecosystem biomass rises by more 
than 10% (Figure 4.4). In this case, the loss of profits and employment affects both the semi-
industrial and the artisanal fleet. 
 
 

76 
4.3.2 Fishing policy search (Scenarios 4 – 5) 
4.3.2.1 Single-objective optimizations 
Results of the single-objective optimization search are presented in Figure 4.5a, which displays 
the relative changes of each objective for each run optimizing one of the management goals. 
The benefits for these goals are not balanced and the maximization of economic profit is not 
compatible with any conservation goals, whereas the social value can be combined with 
biodiversity. While shrimp trawling is linked to high profits, it is not favorable in the scenarios 
promoting ecosystem health and biodiversity. The relative changes in fleet effort for each 
management scenario suggested by the fishing policy search routine are presented in Figure 
4.5b. Severe effort changes are suggested for single-objective scenarios. To maximize social 
value, for instance, an 8 times increase of effort of the manual collection and the sardine purse-
seine fleet and a 6 times increase of shrimp trawling effort is suggested. The handline fleet and 
manual collection are in better agreement with biodiversity. All scenarios, except for 
insufficient gillnet effort reduction, cause a severe loss of profit to the semi-industrial fishing 
sector. At the same time, it provides an opportunity for the artisanal fleet to increase catch and 
profits. To maximize the ecosystem health, strict effort limitations for all fleets are necessary, 
the same holds for the mandated rebuilding of all functional groups to a biomass level from 25 
years ago, causing high social and economic costs. The rebuilding process would entail severe 
profit declines for all fleets, except the longline fleet.

management objectives (fill), as a result of maximizing one of the management objectives 4 (single-objective 
optimization) or several objectives 5 (multi-objective compromises). B: Suggested relative fishing effort for each 
fleet (fill) in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) by the policy search, in order to maximize different management 
goals (scenarios).

4.3.2.2 Multi-objective optimizations 
The two compromises are considered trade-offs between all optimization goals, including both 
socio-economic and ecosystem conservation aspects. Both compromises manage to minimize 
losses to the social value and/or profit, while improving biodiversity and ecosystem health in 
the Gulf of Nicoya. Compromise A and B are compared in the summary table of all scenarios 
(Table 4.6)

4.3.2.3 Compromise A 
Compromise A focuses on increasing ecosystem health (+10%), as well as increasing 
biodiversity, with marginal over-all losses of profits (loss of profit to the semi-industrial fleet), 
but causing substantial social cost (-15%) (Figure 4.6a). The measures of effort control are 
balanced among the artisanal and semi-industrial fishing sector (Figure 4.6b). In this 
compromise, we allow the purse-seiners to continue with only 20% of their original effort. 
Shrimp trawlers would be allowed to operate with up to 50% of their original effort. It is 
important to note that this is the outcome of a theoretical modelling routine. The shrimp trawling 
has already been banned and our modelling analysis shows that it is a successful and efficient 
policy measure. The compromise assumes effort reduction for each artisanal fleet (decrease 
between 10-55% each). 
78 
4.3.2.4 Compromise B 
A second compromise (B) increases future ecosystem health and biodiversity without severe 
social costs and loss of livelihoods for the artisanal fishermen. It entails profit losses of 20% 
mostly at the expenses of the semi-industrial fleet (Figure 4.6a). Compromise B maintains the 
ban on shrimp trawling, combined with a ban on purse-seine fishing in the entire gulf, only 
maintaining artisanal fishing activities. An additional reduction of longline fishing effort is 
suggested to further promote a recovery of predatory species (Figure 4.6b). Compromise B can 
obtain the second highest increase in overall ecosystem biomass by 6% (Figure 4.4) and the 
recovery of a majority of functional groups, including shrimp, small pelagics, large drums and 
snook as well as other predatory species. Benthic species are not showing a positive effect in 
any tested scenario. 
 
4.4 Discussion 
4.4.1 Complexity of policy management approach 
In many marine systems there is a variety of stakeholders who are linked to fisheries along the 
economic supply chain (Jacinto and Pomeroy 2011), which make fisheries management 
scientifically and socially complex (Hare 2020). To face the challenge of overfishing in many 
marine areas, according to (Beddington et al. 2005) integrated management framework are 
required that include co–management and collective decision–making are needed. To provide 
these information in a holistic manner, we followed the Ecosystem Approach to Fisheries (EAF) 
by defining management strategies that consider the ecosystem as a whole, with its main species 
and the trophic interactions between them, as well as the effects of fishing (FAO 2012). We 
aimed to find a balance between ecosystem and biodiversity conservation by rebuilding biomass 
of key species on the one hand and socio-economic aspects and stakeholder interests on the 
other hand.  
In the Gulf of Nicoya, the Costa Rican fisheries hotspot, a complex range of stakeholders are 
involved. On the one hand, there is the semi-industrial fleet that operates in the gulf, fishing 
with purse-seine nets which target small pelagic fish as well as the shrimp trawlers that targeted 
different inshore and offshore shrimp species (until 2019). On the other hand, artisanal fishing 
fleets operate in the gulf, using a diverse set of gears (handline, longline, gillnets, traps, manual 
bivalve extraction) and catching a variety of species.  
Women of the fishing communities contribute to small-scale artisanal fishing, mainly the 
collection of bivalves, and most importantly to a variety of pre- and post-harvest activities, that 
continue to be poorly recognized (Cruz et al. 2022). Their contribution needs to be especially 
considered in the development of policy plans.  
4.4.2 Efficiency of the ban on trawling (current policy) 
The first step of our assessment of the management scenarios tested the current policy, the ban 
on shrimp trawling. We can state from our modelling results, that a severe reduction of shrimp 
trawling is essential for the recovery of the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem. All tested management 
scenarios that include the goals of increasing ecosystem health and biodiversity, suggest a 
79 
reduction of trawling by 50-100%. Modelling results imply that the existing shrimp trawling 
ban will have several positive effects on the recent and future ecosystem health. 
As a result of the ban on trawling, firstly, we would expect an increase of shrimp biomass. The 
speed of recovery strongly depends on the current status of the stock. From examples of the 
past, we know that it can be a difficult and protracted process. In the case of the Indonesian 
shrimp fishery, for example, the over-fished shrimp stocks showed a recovery within a 7-year 
period after the ban (Chong et al. 1987). In Hong Kong waters first signs of recovery were 
visible 3,5 years after the trawl ban, however the biomass remained unchanged for the next 5 
years after the ban. Furthermore, the ban on trawling will have a positive effect on the bycatch 
species, ocean bottom flora and fauna, and demersal bottom-associated species. The recovery 
of bycatch populations, such as small demersal fish species, can be faster than the increase of 
shrimp biomass, as has been shown in an embayment in Kenya, (Munga et al. 2012) and in 
(estuarine) coastal waters of Hong Kong, China (Mak et al. 2021). We expect a recovery of 
vulnerable bycatch species, such as eel (Cynoponticus coniceps, Brotula clarkae) and flatfish, 
both frequently caught as bycatch by the shrimp-trawling fishery (Herrera et al. 2016). 
Increasing abundance of lower trophic level species will most likely be followed by the increase 
in biomass of longer-lived predatory species, like drums and snook, mackerel, barracuda and 
sharks. Especially demersal sharks and rays are a vulnerable bycatch species in the trawl 
fisheries of the Gulf of Nicoya (Clarke et al. 2016).  
Despite the benefits of the ban on trawling, we see from our modelling results that this recent 
policy is presumably not sufficient to achieve a full recovery of biomass levels over all 
functional groups. The ecosystem has strongly degraded over the last 30 years, and more severe 
measures are necessary to turn the tide. Given the fact that the catch of the artisanal fleet would 
likely increase in the future, due to the loss of competition from the shrimp trawlers, we believe 
that some additional restrictions are necessary. One also has to keep in mind that a full recovery 
of all species in the system is not very likely to be achieved simultaneously due to the variety 
of trophic-interactions and other complex external influences (Froese et al. 2016; Steven 
Mackinson et al. 2009).  
4.4.3 Stakeholder engagement in the decision-making process 
Furthermore, there is often a gap between stakeholder interests and ecological knowledge 
(Bethel et al. 2014; Evans 2010), that can be bridged by involving stakeholders in the decision-
making process. In our study we were able to show how the Gulf of Nicoya EwE model can be 
used to initiate a discussion with engaged stakeholders about appropriate management of target 
species in the ecosystem. As shown in several workshops (Sánchez-Jiménez et al. 2019) , the 
fishermen have fundamental knowledge about past and present resource availability in their 
fishing areas. With this knowledge, EwE simulations could be used to present and visualize 
possible scenarios of the future development of resource availability and the consequences and 
benefits of effort control. One outcome of these workshops was that according to the fishermen, 
gillnet effort should be reduced (by 25%) to rebuild the stock biomass of key resources. 
However, our study revealed that a gillnet effort reduction as the only measure (without a 
concurrent trawling ban) is not powerful enough in terms of ecosystem conservation but causes 
a significant loss of profit to the artisanal fishermen.  
80 
We rather recommend to strengthen the enforcement and control mechanisms of illegal mesh 
sizes (smaller than 6.35 cm [2.5 in]), to let juvenile target species reach the age of maturity and 
to allow population sizes to increase. This is also suggested by Fonseca et al. (2005), who state 
that mesh sizes larger than 60-80 mm have a better compatibility with sustainable management 
strategies. We suggest that stakeholder engagement can be particularly intensified in the entire 
EwE modelling processes starting with the data collection, continuing with the model 
development and model validation and lastly end with management simulations. As it has been 
shown in several cases, stakeholder engagement can be used for management strategy 
evaluation (Fulton, Jones, et al. 2011) and is a key aspect in providing good governance, to 
dissolve top-down hierarchies and to improve the compliance of all actors (Pita et al. 2010; 
Soma 2003). 
4.4.4 Policy Search – is there another more effective fishing policy? 
We analyzed the results of the fishing policy search (EwE) to suggest an optimized policy plan. 
The results highlight the discrepancy between socio-economic and conservational goals and the 
challenge of finding the right balance for policy-making. Increasing profits come with 
increasing semi-industrial fishing efforts at the expense of ecosystem health. Effort reduction 
of the artisanal fleet in favor of ecosystem health entails the loss of jobs for the fishermen in 
the communities around the Gulf of Nicoya. 
We ran an additional scenario, the mandated rebuilding of species, to present the measures that 
would be necessary to restore biomass levels of functional groups to the known state of 1993 
(baseline model by Alms and Wolff (2019); Wolff et al. (1998) ), disregarding the feasibility 
and social-economic aspects. In this scenario the extent of ecosystem degradation is 
highlighted. Necessary measures would involve a ban on trawling, and a reduction of gillnet, 
handline and purse-seine effort to a minimum. The scenario would only allow the longline fleet 
to operate in the Gulf of Nicoya (-10%), together with manual collection (-20%), leading to an 
80% loss of profit and social value. 
Since this scenario is not viable, we looked for compromises from both multi-objective 
optimizations runs, that are trade-offs between the four management objectives. One 
compromise (A) is oriented slightly more towards maintaining social-economic benefits 
(limiting effort reduction for each fleet), while the other compromise (B) focuses on ecosystem 
health and biodiversity conservation. After the assessment of our modelling results, we 
conclude that compromise B has the best long-term outcomes. This scenario suggests keeping 
the existing ban on shrimp trawling, supplemented by the ban on purse-seine fishing. This 
scenario would allow only artisanal fishing activities in the Gulf of Nicoya. All artisanal fleets 
can maintain their recent effort, except for a 20% reduction of longline effort, which is an 
additional measure to allow for long-term recovery of the top predators, such as rays and sharks. 
One major advantage of this policy option is that the obstacle of implementing the ban on 
shrimp trawling has already been overcome. Compromise B will surely lead to a substantial 
loss of profit to the fishing industry compared to prior to the trawling ban. However, one needs 
to keep in mind that the shrimp industry had collapsed already due to the overfishing of inshore 
shrimp species and deep-water shrimp, followed by declining resources (-50% biomass) (Alms 
and Wolff 2019). Decreasing biomass of target resources resulted in lower CPUE, which 
reduced the profit and income of the fishing vessels due to a high level of effort and costs 
81 
relative to the value of the harvested catch (FAO 2020; Froese et al. 2018) . This tremendous 
loss of profit to the fisheries (-50%) occurred between 1993 and 2013 (Alms and Wolff 2019). 
While the sardine purse-seine fleet would surely suffer from the suggested ban, it might still be 
early enough to prevent a similar collapse of the small pelagic fish population in and around 
the Gulf of Nicoya. In the long run, after a recovery of the small pelagic target species, the 
purse-seiners could gradually be allowed back to operate in the outer parts of the Gulf of 
Nicoya. Thus, this short‐term burden could lead to a long-term increase in profits (Brown et al. 
2015; Froese et al. 2018). It is known that the semi-industrial fishing fleets compete with 
artisanal fishing practice (Stiles et al. 2010) and the industrial purse-seine fisheries may impact 
upon artisanal and subsistence fishers (Leroy et al. 2016). Both semi-industrial fleets remove 
great amounts of biomass from the mid-trophic levels. In the case of the Gulf of Nicoya, these 
groups are small pelagic fish and several shrimp species, which have been identified as keystone 
species and important links between trophic levels (Alms and Wolff 2019). The collapse of 
more key target fish populations needs to be prevented, to sustain fishing resources for the 
artisanal fleet, as fishing provides basic income and food security, with rare options for 
alternative livelihoods available (Proyecto Golfos 2013). The compromise B favors the 
artisanal sector and most likely even allows for the artisanal fleets to increase catches and/or 
CPUE. This can be an advantage for its implementation. 
The alternative compromise (A) reduces the impact on the industrial fleet, increasingly 
impacting the artisanal fleet. In this compromise, we allow the purse-seiners to continue with 
only 20% of its original effort. The shrimp trawlers would be allowed to operate at 50% of its 
original effort. However, as the shrimp trawling has already been banned and the modelling 
analysis shows the positive effect it has, we strongly recommend maintaining the ban. The 
overall biomass cannot be increased as effectively as in compromise B. The compromise puts 
effort reductions on each artisanal fleet (decrease between 10-55% each). Critical aspects of 
compromise A are the social consequences and the loss of jobs, which also affect artisanal 
fishermen, who would have to either find alternative occupation or rely on state subsidies, 
which are often given due to a lack of alternative employment. In this case fishermen are 
encouraged to stay in the fishing industry, despite the lack of resources (Sumaila et al. 2010). 
Ideally, policies that facilitate alternative income opportunities can be provided, similar to what 
has been done in other coastal regions of the world. Examples of alterative occupation options 
established for fishers being left out after fleets reduction include aquaculture (Ghana, Anning 
et al. 2012), the farming of shrimps (Brazil, Cavalli et al. 2008), or the engagement in touristic 
activities, such as recreational fishing trips/sea trips offered by the fishermen (Porter and Orams 
2014). In Costa Rica artisanal fishermen collecting bivalves have been alternatively employed 
for the restoration of mangroves, not only providing a livelihood but ecosystem services to fish, 
shrimp, and society. Same actions have been taken in other marine areas like India, Malaysia, 
Nigeria and Panama (BDCP 2007; Porter and Orams 2014; Suman 2019; Woods et al. 2022). 
According to Johnson et al. (2021), the collapse of ecosystem services, such as marine fisheries, 
could result in a dramatic decline of the global GDP in the next decade. At the same time, the 
preservation of the ecosystem health is a major factor for economic growth. Thus, short-term 
economic losses due to fishing effort reductions to allow for ecosystem recovery might be 
preferrable. Eventually, a successful management plan will pay off in terms of higher catch 
82 
rates, increase in (financially) valuable species and higher catches as well as less effort for 
artisanal fishermen. 
As it has been stated by Coglan and Pascoe (2014), fishermen have to handle a high amount of 
uncertainty concerning their future catch rates, prices and costs. With predictive models we 
have the right tools at hand to develop reliable fisheries management plans that minimize these 
livelihood-threatening uncertainties. Ideally, these processes are fulfilled in cooperation with 
relevant stakeholders. For a suitable management strategy that regulates the fishing effort for 
each fleet, one has to consider that the ecosystem will be additionally affected by climate 
variations and other external factors, such as pollution or change in the land use (FAO 2020). 
As it has been shown by Alms and Wolff (2020), particularly during El Niño periods with 
extreme Sea Surface Temperatures and increased precipitation, decreasing phytoplankton 
productivity and zooplankton biomass are expected to occur with bottom–up effects on the 
entire food chain. Thus, a stringent effort control, that incorporates a buffer for external effects, 
will help the ecosystem to recover and to increase its resilience towards climate change in the 
future. 
 
4.5 Conclusion 
The here presented modelling routine is a useful tool to explore management options and inform 
stakeholders about the benefits of specific policy measures, and the results of our assessment 
can serve as the basis for management advice. However, modelling results always include some 
uncertainties and as it is highlighted by Christensen and Walters (2004b), models cannot fully 
represent natural dynamics. According to Plagányi and Butterworth (2004) the limitation of the 
EwE approach lies in the quality and quantity of available data. The model is balanced and fit 
to time-series to reduce uncertainty, but the Ecosim modelling tool as well as the policy search 
are sensitive to the parameter settings used. Thus, it is recommended to confirm the results 
through further analysis, before the model results are directly applied to policymaking. The 
EwE plug-in Ecosampler could be used to measure the impact of the input parameter sensitivity 
on the modelling results e.g. for the use of time-series simulation using Ecosim (Steenbeek et 
al. 2018). Other options to validate the results, could be stock assessment or bioeconomic and 
socio-economic analysis. 
Following the EAF guidelines, after the implementation of the selected policy measures a 
monitoring system is needed, followed by a phase of assessing the data and possibly revising 
the policy plan (FAO 2012). Furthermore, the ban on trawling is a rare and valuable opportunity 
for a "before - after” comparison to assess the recovery in ecosystem structure and function 
after the removal of a major distraction (Tao et al. 2021). 
The goals of the Ocean Decade are numerous and several next steps of producing scientific 
knowledge for our study area in Costa Rica and Latin America are relevant. One of the goals is 
a “mapped and protected marine ecosystem”. With that, the location of possible MPAs and/or 
predestined areas of seasonal closure (veda), can be assessed through a spatial model using the 
Ecospace tool, the spatial-temporal component of Ecopath with Ecosim. It could be assessed 
how the “veda” can be used best for conservation and whether it is necessary to exclude all 
83 
fishing fleets from the region, depending also on other policy measures implemented in the Gulf 
of Nicoya. 
 
Acknowledgments 
The facilities in Costa Rica and some of the data about the Gulf of Nicoya were provided by 
the Centro de Investigación en Ciencias del Mar y Limnología (CIMAR) and the Universidad 
de Costa Rica. The research project was strongly supported by our main project partner Alvaro 
Morales Ramírez in Costa Rica, who provided the infrastructure and guidance in Costa Rica 
and helped to develop the project collaboration between the Leibniz Centre for Tropical Marine 
Research and the CIMAR. INCOPESCA supported the study by providing fisheries statistics 
and guidance concerning the marine resources in the Gulf of Nicoya.

A SPATIAL ECOSYSTEM MODEL TO ASSESS THE EFFICIENCY 
OF MARTINE PROTECTION PLANS 
  
85 
Chapter 5 
 
A Spatial Ecosystem Model to assess the Efficiency of Marine Protection Plans 
Viola Alms, Giovanni Romagnoni, and Matthias Wolff 
 
This is a draft manuscript in preparation for submission. 
 
Contribution statement 
Project idea:      Viola Alms 
Design of the work:    Viola Alms supported by Giovanni Romagnoni 
Acquisition or interpretation of data:  Viola Alms 
Drafted manuscript revised by Giovanni Romagnoni and Matthias Wolff. 
  
86 
Abstract 
The Gulf of Nicoya, located at the Pacific coast of Costa Rica, is a highly productive estuary 
and the country’s primary fishing region. Previous assessment using holistic trophic models to 
examine the ecosystem’s status revealed an overfished and degraded system, with decreasing 
biomass of valuable target species. The ecosystem degradation was mainly driven by intensive 
fishing, especially by the semi-industrial shrimp trawling fleet that operated in the gulf until 
2019. Besides a trawling ban, an annual temporary closed season, which existed for many years 
was implemented as another countermeasure against depletion. However, the regulations 
governing this fishing closure have recently been relaxed.  
This study tested both the effects of removing trawling on the ecosystem and the desired 
benefits, the temporary Marine Protected Area (MPA) has on the target species. The goal is to 
search for conservation measures that are needed to ensure the efficiency of the fisheries 
closure. 
To achieve this, the study utilized Ecospace, a spatial modelling tool that is part of the Ecopath 
with Ecosim (EwE) software suite. With Ecospace, species biomass and fishing effort can be 
analyzed over space and time, with results visualized through maps. 
Findings from our previous study identified trawling ban from the gulf, as an important 
measure, allowing various species to recover. In our model, we observed significant recovery 
of bycatch species and upper-trophic-level species. However, rebuilding the shrimp species 
biomass appears to require either more time or more protective measures.  
The 3 months closed season is an important conservation measure for many species in the Gulf 
of Nicoya; however, our model simulations suggest room for improvement. Extending the 
fishing closure to encompass the entire gulf and apply it to all fishing fleets would significantly 
increase its efficiency. Moreover, including the artisanal manual collection into the closure 
should be considered, as in our simulations all its target species show a declining biomass over 
time, until 2039. 
This study shows how spatial models can be used to simulate and visualise processes of spatio-
temporal change in the ecosystem and how existing fishing closures can be checked for its 
efficiency.   
Keywords: MPA, fishing closure, spatial modelling, Ecopath with Ecosim, shrimp trawling 
  
87 
5.1 Introduction 
The Gulf of Nicoya is a productive estuary and the hotspot for the Costa Rican fisheries, which 
has been subject to overexploitation for many decades, especially caused by the semi-industrial 
shrimp trawlers. Since the early 1990s, the ecosystem continuously degraded, with many 
declines in target species catch, causing a substantial economic loss (Alms and Wolff 2019). 
Like other coastal ecosystems (Blaber 2002; Del Solar  at al. 2024; Trégarot et al. 2024) , the 
Gulf of Nicoya is not only impacted by its fisheries, but also by anthropogenic activities, 
pollution and climate change (Vargas 2016). To address these concerns, Costa Rican authorities 
have implemented conservation measures to regulate fishing, such as spatial and temporary 
restrictions in specific areas of the Gulf of Nicoya and a ban on bottom trawling since 2019.  
Holistic ecosystem modelling emerged as a key tool to evaluate the effectiveness of these 
conservational measures and to provide a valuable basis for management advice (Fulton et al. 
2011). This is especially crucial in data-poor regions, where the lack of integrated ecosystem-
based information limits the development of policies and marine management plans (Feary et 
al. 2014). Globally, ecological information is needed to achieve the goals proclaimed by the 
United Nations (UN). ‘The Decade of Ocean Science for Sustainable Development’ (2021–
2030) aims to achieve a healthy, resilient and sustainably harvested ocean for many costal 
countries worldwide (Heymans et al. 2020; Ryabinin et al. 2019) . While the UN aimed to 
preserve 10% of the ocean by 2020, the current High Seas Treaty aims to convert 30% of the 
seas into protected areas by 2030 (United Nations). To achieve these goals, suitable areas for 
protection need to be identified in many regions of the world. However, the efficiency of MPAs 
significantly relies on the right choice of location, the spatial and temporal extent as well as 
successful enforcement (Dimarchopoulou et al. 2024).  
Ecosystem food webs are dynamic, due to changes in natural predator-prey interactions, 
resource limitations and anthropogenic influences, or due to environmental influences 
(Frederiksen et al. 2006; Maureaud et al. 2017; Pinnegar et al. 2000). Also, ecosystems are 
spatially heterogeneous, with species abundance and productivity varying across regions 
(Hernvann et al. 2020). Here, Ecospace emerges as a useful tool. It is a spatial modelling tool 
embedded in the Ecopath with Ecosim software suite. It can be used to explore spatial 
management options and to address various research question, including the regional 
consequences of climate change (Christensen and Walters 2004a). It can valuably incorporate 
temporal and spatial factors in the analysis as well as defining fisheries restricted areas. Though 
the tool is less widely used than other EwE software components, its importance is increasingly 
recognized, especially in terms of the recent UN endeavors (Mutsert et al. 2024). 
Prior studies using Ecopath with Ecosim (Alms et al. 2022), suggested a ban on shrimp trawling 
as a necessary measure to allow ecosystem recovery. Simulations showed that the ban could 
lead to the desired long-term recovery of shrimp biomass, along with increases in biomasses of 
bycatch species and predatory species, as well as an overall increase in the total ecosystem 
biomass. After decades of intensive exploitation of shrimp species and associated bycatch, the 
shrimp trawlers were effectively banned from the Gulf of Nicoya since 2019. In this study, we 
hereby evaluate the local consequences of this ban on species distribution and fishing efforts in 
the gulf using the Ecospace model. While shrimp stock recovery depends on the stock status, it 
88 
can be a challenging and a lengthy process (e.g. Chong et al. 1987; Munga et al. 2012). We also 
simulate the changes in the distribution of the bycatch species, which tend to recover faster than 
the shrimps itself (Munga et al. 2012), and look at the bottom-up effects on the predatory 
species. The restoration of shrimp biomass and small demersal fish biomass (as bycatch species) 
will likely cascade through the system, and positively affect the upper trophic levels. Thus, we 
model the recovery phase after a prolonged intensive trawling using the Ecospace software. 
Our first goal is to simulate the efficiency of the existing seasonal fishing closure in certain 
areas of the Gulf of Nicoya. The closure, known as ‘veda’, is an annual three-monthly closure 
on all fishing fleets, in the inner and intermediate Gulf of Nicoya, except for manual collection, 
which has been active for decades. The first closure was established in 1985 for trammel nets 
only, then further extended, including trawling, purse seine and gillnets. The regulations have 
undergone further adjustment over the years, since the three-month closure (from May to July) 
was established in 1990.  Since then, the fishing closure occurs every year to reduce fishing 
effort and allow certain species to reproduce, grow and recover from the intense fishing 
pressure. The closure aligns with spawning seasons of commercially important species, such as 
snapper ( Lutjanus spp.), Sciaenidae ( Cynoscion spp.), jacks and pompanos, catfish ( Bagre 
spp.), small demersals (Mullidae), small pelagics ( Anchoa spp., Cetengraulis spp., among 
others), snook (Centropomus), mackerel and barracuda ( Scomberomorus sierra, Sphyraena 
ensis), camarón blanco (Penaeus) and crabs (Callinectes). We aimed to simulate the 
consequences of the fishing closure for the spatial distribution of species and fishing fleet effort. 
Most small artisanal fishermen are unable to fish during the closed season, while the advanced 
fleet is believed to partly shift to the outer gulf.  An additional aspect of our analysis is the 
assessment of the importance of certain regulations of the fishing closure. In recent years, some 
changes were made to the regulations of the fishing closure. Since the Covid-19 pandemic, the 
fisheries institute Instituto Costarricense de Pesca y Acuicultura (INCOPESCA) has eased the 
regulations, allowing semi-industrial sardine vessels to fish for two of the three months in the 
closed area. It remains to be verified whether these changes affect the potential success of the 
fishing closure in any way. Our research addresses the following key questions about the 
closure’s regulation: Is it necessary to close the whole gulf for all fleets? Is it advisable that the 
manual collection of molluscs and crustaceans is exempt from the ban? Is there a need for 
temporal or spatial extension of the fishing closure? These research questions are tackled by 
the modelling analysis.  
 
5.2 Material and Methods 
5.2.1 Study area 
The Gulf of Nicoya (10◦N, 85◦W) is a tidally influenced estuary on the northwest Pacific coast 
of Costa Rica (Figure 5.1). The model area spans 1530 km 2, from the northern inland (mouth 
of the Tempisque River) to the Pacific Ocean entrance in the south. According to its shape and 
bathymetry, the Gulf of Nicoya is divided into three regions (Figure 5.1): inner gulf (zone 201), 
intermediate gulf (zone 202) and outer gulf zone 203 (Vargas 2016). The division is based on 
the species composition and fishing gears used, as defined by the Costa Rican institute of 
fisheries (INCOPESCA) (Sánchez-Jiménez et al. 2019). 
89 
The shallow interior zone (depth < 10 m) of the Gulf is characterized by shallow waters and 
tidally influenced mud- and sandflats. It is fringed by mangroves and dominated by the seasonal 
freshwater inflow from the Tempisque River (Proyecto Golfos 2012). The inner gulf is 
considered a high-priority area due to its importance as a reproduction and nursery ground for 
commercially important fish and shrimp species (Alpízar 2011; Proyecto Golfos 2012; Vargas 
2016). The intermediate zone of the gulf is mostly surrounded by sandy beaches and mangroves. 
Here, the physical parameters of the water masses vary due to intense mixing between the 
oceanic water and freshwater from both adjacent zones (Proyecto Golfos 2012b). This region 
comprises reproduction areas for small pelagic fish species (Clupeiformes: Clupeidae and 
Engraulidae), shrimps Litopenaeus spp . (Decapoda: Penaeidae), snappers Lutjanus spp.  
(Perciformes: Lutjanidae) and drums Cynoscion spp. (Perciformes: Sciaenidae). It also serves 
as feeding grounds for several commercially targeted species, such as the Mexican Barracuda 
Sphyraena ensis (Perciformes: Sphyraena). The outer oceanic zone extends from a minimum 
depth of 20 m to 200 m at the shelf edge, surrounded by sandy beaches and rocky shores (Wolff 
et al. 1998). This zone is mainly influenced by oceanic waters and the Tárcoles River input. 
The eastern region of the outer gulf is a hatching and reproduction site for penaeid shrimps, 
sharks and several other fish species (Proyecto Golfos 2012; Vargas 2016). Like i n other 
tropical coastal estuarine areas, mangroves in the Gulf of Nicoya contribute to primary 
production, by providing organic matter and play an important role in enhancing fish and 
invertebrate productivity; thus, significantly supporting the local fis heries (Clarke et al. 2016; 
Hutchison et al. 2014; Wehrtmann and Cortés 2008; Wolff 2006). Due to its high productivity, 
the estuary is the country’s principal fishing ground for both the artisanal fishing fleet (Vargas, 
1995) and the semi-industrial fishing fleets, thereby, providing approximately 55% of the total 
catch in the Gulf of Nicoya (in 2013). The small-scale artisanal fishing sector is socially and 
economically important  for approximately 2000 fishermen and over 20 coastal communities 
(Lozano and Heinen 2016a; Trujillo et al. 2012). Four artisanal fishing fleets operate in the gulf 
using different boat sizes and gear types to target various demersal and coastal species 
(BIOMARCC-SINAC-GIZ 2013a). Gillnets are predominantly used, followed by the longline, 
handline and manual mollusk extraction, which is mostly conducted by women from the coastal 
communities, synergizing with the manual trap fisheries targeting different crustaceans. 
The main port of the Gulf of Nicoya, Puntarenas, is also the shrimp trawlers landing site for the 
semi-industrial fishing fleets, the purse-seine fleet and the shrimp trawl fleet. The purse-seine 
vessels target small pelagic fish species ( Anchoa spp., Cetengraulis mysticetus  and 
Opisthonema spp.) in the intermediate and outer zones of the Gulf of Nicoya. The bottom trawl 
fleet mostly targeted several shrimp species in the outer gulf; but unfortunately, the capture of 
bycatch species drastically exceeded the shrimp catches (Clarke et al. 2016). 
90 
 
Figure 5.1 - Maps of the study area (Gulf of Nicoya, Costa Rica) , from Alms and Wolff (2020). 
5.2.2 Modelling background 
5.2.2.1 Food-web Model – Ecopath 
For the modeling task, the open-source software Ecopath with Ecosim (EwE) versions 6.6.5 
(Christensen and Walters 2004) was used. The Ecopath approach has been widely used to assess 
and visualize structural changes within ecosystems over time (Pauly, Christensen, and Walters 
2000). Ecopath with Ecosim operates on a set of linear equations for each functional group in 
the ecosystem, connecting the group’s biomass production to other groups through a prey–
predator matrix. For each compartment (i), the basic equation is as follows: 
𝑃௜ = 𝑌௜ + 𝐵௜𝑀2௜ + 𝐸௜ + 𝐵𝑎௜ + 𝑃௜(1 − 𝐸𝐸) 
where Pi is the total production rate of i; Yi is the total fishery catch rate; M2i is the instantaneous 
predation rate of group i; Bi is the biomass; E i is the net migration rate (emigration–
immigration); BAi is the biomass accumulation rate; Pi(1 − EE) represents the “other mortality” 
rate of i; and EE is the ecotrophic efficiency of i and the proportion of the production that is 
utilized in the system (Christensen and Walters 2004; Christensen et al. 2005). 
While equation 1 allows for the balancing of the biomass production in each group with its use 
in the system (and the fishery), a second equation allows for estimating the energy budget of 
each group i as follows: 
𝐶𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑢𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛௜ = 𝑃𝑟𝑜𝑑𝑢𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛௜ + 𝑅𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑖𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛௜ + (𝐸𝑔𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 𝑜𝑓 𝑢𝑛𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝑓𝑜𝑜𝑑)௜ 
The model was built around the main groups targeted by the fisheries, while grouping species 
with similar sizes, diets, consumption, mortality, production rates and habitats. In total, the 
model comprised 22 functional groups, including 3 primary producer groups and 10 different 
fish groups.  
The balanced trophic model of the Gulf of Nicoya by Alms and Wolff (2019) was used as a 
basis for our further modelling approach. This EwE model of the Gulf of Nicoya was built 
around the main groups targeted by the fisheries. Species with similar sizes, diets, consumption, 
mortality, production rates and habitats were grouped, representing the state of the ecosystem 
in 2013. To construct the spatial model ( EwE Ecospace) and better capture spatial patterns of 

91 
interest, we made modifications, including dividing the shrimp functional group into two 
categories: coastal shrimp (Penaeus spp., Farfantepenaeus spp., Trachypenaeus sp.) and deep-
water shrimp (Solenocera sp., Heterocarpus sp.), according to their preferred depth ranges. To 
split the functional group according to the selected species, we used INCOPESCA landings 
data and additional information from Wehrtmann and Nielsen-(Muñoz 2009). The P/B and Q/B 
were adjusted for each shrimp group separately, considering their physiology and using 
information from other studies in similar areas and ecosystems (García and Duarte 2002; 
Giarrizzo et al. 2013; Wehrtmann and Nielsen-Muñoz 2009). 
Our updated model comprises of 23 functional groups, including 3 primary producer groups 
and 10 different fish groups (Table 5.1). The model was balanced and fitted to time-series data, 
including historic fisheries catches, recent monitoring data and effort data. For further details 
please refer to Alms and Wolff (2020).

consumption-to-biomass ratio (Qi/Bi; year−1), ecotrophic efficiency (EEi), production-to-consumption ratio (Pi/Qi) 
and total catch (metric tons⋅km− 2 ⋅year−1 ) are presented for each functional group.
