5.2.2.2 The Spatial Model – Ecospace 
Ecospace can be used for the simulation of the spatial distribution of species and fishing fleet 
effort over time. it is particularly useful for exploring spatial management options and 
addressing various research questions, such as the regional consequences of climate change 
(Christensen and Walters 2004a; Mutsert et al. 2024). Ecospace models  are spatially explicit, 
time dynamic models based on the  Ecopath mass-balance and Ecosim time dynamic routines 
(Christensen and Walters 2024). A set of differential equations is defined based on the biomass 
components of change for consumer functional groups, expressed as: 
𝑑𝐵௜
𝑑𝑡 = 𝑔௜∙෍ 𝑄௝௜
௡
௝ୀଵ
−෍ 𝑄௜௝
௡
௝ୀଵ
+ 𝐼௜− (𝐹௜௧+ 𝑒௜+ 𝑀0௜௧) ∙ 𝐵௜௧ 
where Bit is the biomass of i at time t, gi is the growth efficiency, Ii is the immigration rate; Fit is 
the mortality rate due to harvesting (fishing mortality); ei is the emigration rate; and M0i the 
other mortality. The terms Qji and Qij represent the consumption due to predation by j on i, and 
by i on j, respectively. For primary producers, the consumption rate term is replaced by a 
production rate function f (Bit)) represents primary production rate as a function of the group 
biomass (Christensen and Steenbeek 2024). 
The Ecospace model is defined as a basemap based on a two-dimensional grid, where each cell 
is designated as land or water, and each cell can have unique characteristics (Christensen and 
Steenbeek 2024). For our basemap, an area of 1530 km2 was defined using an 80x76 grid, with 
1 km2 grid cells. To facilitate regional analysis, the Gulf of Nicoya was divided into three 
regions, as defined by the fishing authorities: the inner gulf, intermediate gulf and outer gulf 
(zones 201,202 and 203). The map also includes the primary port known as Puntarenas, which 
serves the semi-industrial and longline fleet, along with smaller ports mainly used by the 
artisanal fleet (Figure 5.2). 
 a)      b) 
 
 
  

93 
c)      d) 
 
 
Figure 5.2 -  Ecospace basemap of the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica)with the main ports: a) displaying the 
bathymetry from shallow waters in light blue to deeper waters in dark blue (5-200m) and the three zones (201, 
202 and 203). b) the distribution of habitat types (see legend), c) primary productivity as relative value low (light 
green) to high (dark green) d) Salinity form low values in light blue to higher values in red (8-34 ppm).  
In the basemap, we defined 9 different habitat types, differentiated by a combination of bottom 
type (sand, mud, rocks) and depth zones (coastal, neritic, oceanic). Additionally, we determined 
the mangrove forest habitat, mainly located in the inner and intermediate gulf, mainly consisting 
of Rhizophora sp., Pelliciera sp. and Avicennia sp. We distinguish between the inner and outer 
mangrove forest. The outer mangrove area is a feeding area for different species, whereas the 
dense inner mangrove forest is more challenging for certain species to enter (small pelagics, 
flatfish). It serves as an area of shelter for shrimps, lobster, crabs, bivalves. The distribution of 
these habitat types is presented in Figure 5.2b.  
Further maps of the spatial distribution of different parameters were created for the Gulf of 
Nicoya. The water depth reaches from 5 m in the inner gulf close to the mouth of the river 
Tempisque up to 200 m in the outer oceanic part of the gulf (Figure 5.2a). The primary 
productivity is highest around the mouth of the river Tempisque and in coastal areas close to 
other river inputs (Figure 5.2c). Salinity was defined as values between 8–34 ppm displayed in 
Figure 5.2d. The Gulf of Nicoya consists of brackish water at the mouth of the Tempisque river 

94 
in the inner Gulf of and of oceanic water in the outer gulf. The spatial environmental 
information (depth, salinity and primary productivity) is based on a GIS database conducted by 
the CIMAR/University of San José, Costa Rica (unpublished data). 
To model the distribution of species, defining the spatial distribution within the ecosystem of 
each functional group is necessary. The model operates based on the habitat capacity model 
(Christensen and Steenbeek 2024; Mutsert et al. 2024), where species distribution is driven by 
species preferences for certain factors, such as the habitat type, abiotic environmental factors 
(e.g. salinity or depth) and the prey and predator distribution. This can either be done by 
selecting suitable and unsuitable habitat types for each species and/or by defining the spatial 
distribution through environmental response functions. According to Villy Christensen and Carl 
J. Walters (2024), the environmental response function is often more suitable to describe the 
distribution of the species than the defined habitats only. As environmental responses, we 
defined the optimum depth ranges for certain species (small demersal fish, pelagic fish, shrimps 
and lobster), the abundance related to the distance from the coast (0–19 km) (seabirds) and 
salinity preferences (cephalopods). These information were taken from different studies ( 
Blaber 2008; Briones‐Fourzán and Lozano‐Álvarez 2013; García and Duarte 2002; Guzman et 
al. 2008; Hegde et al., 2016; Hunnam 2021; Schmidt and Dias 2012; Wehrtmann and Nielsen-
Muñoz 2009). Ecospace models describe the biomass of each functional group, within each cell 
in the grid over time by considering trophic interactions, fishing and species’ movement 
(Christensen and Walters 2024). The model calculations are based on several input parameters, 
such as the habitat preference for each functional group and the dispersal rates, indicating the  
average annual movement distances of the functional groups across the ecosystem (Christensen 
et al., 2008). The Dispersal rate is entered as the absolute value of average individual annual 
movement distances due to random movements (Christensen and Steenbeek 2024). Based on 
Christensen and Steenbeek (2024) suggestions, the range of values should be set between 3–
300 km year-1. We assigned low values (3–10 km year-1) to mainly non-dispersing species 
(benthos, lobster, crabs), medium values (30–60 km year-1) to demersal groups (flatfish, small 
demersals, morays & eels) and high values (up to 300 km year-1) to pelagic groups and plankton 
(small pelagics, mackerel & barracuda, rays & sharks, plankton). Some functional groups have 
a low general dispersal rate, such as sessile species (crabs and benthos in general) compared to 
high dispersal rates of pelagic fish species and plankton. The dispersal rate is entered as a base 
rate in the suitable habitat and with adjustments applied when species enter unfavourable 
habitats. We assume that for all species, the vulnerability to predation is higher in unsuitable 
habitats, especially for benthos species, like crabs and lobster. Some fisheries related 
parameters are needed to define the distribution of fleet effort throughout the model area. Basic 
fishing fleet related parameters have been previously defined in the Ecopath and Ecosim model, 
such as the landings, discards and market prices for each functional group. In Ecospace, we 
additionally defined the habitat fishery, identifying which fleet operates in specific habitat 
types. Another important information needed is the definition of costs for each fishing fleet, the 
percentage distribution of effort related costs and fixed costs (Table 5.2). This information is 
needed to determine the fishing costs related to the distance covered by the fleet from port to 
its fishing region. Finally, the effective power set for each fishing fleet is among the key 
parameters governing the effort distribution in Ecospace (Mutsert et al. 2024; Romagnoni et al. 
2015). This scaling factor can be adjusted when a functional group only occur in a small part 
95 
of the model area and serves as a target species for a fleet. In our model, we retained the default 
setting of 2 for the fleets, except for manual collection (1.5), where the distribution of the 
bivalves is mainly located in the mangrove forests.

fleet and the fleet specific number of jobs per catch value in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica).

5.2.2.3 Model Simulation of Conservation Plans 
We assessed the effects of the decline and elimination of shrimp-trawling effort on species in 
the ecosystem using a set of indicators. Coastal and deep-sea shrimp stock biomass was defined 
as one indicator, while the spatial distribution and relative biomass of bycatch and predator 
species were compared between a period of active shrimp trawling to 20 years after the last 
trawler had left the gulf. The goal was to explore whether shrimp and bycatch species would 
show signs of recovery, potentially followed by increased biomass at higher trophic level 
(predator) species. We also compared the fishing effort distribution of all fleets between 2013 
to 2039. 
Ecospace defines fisheries restricted areas (MPAs) that block out fishing from specific 
Ecospace cells in the basemap for a given fleet during a selected time period (Steenbeek and 
Coll 2024). We used this tool to test the effect of the seasonal closure (veda) in the Gulf of 
Nicoya. The fishing closure is a traditional closure of the inner and intermediate gulf (called 
zones 201 and 202, Figure 5.3), where closure is for all fleets (except for artisanal manual 
collection) over a period of 3 months. We tested the effects of the original fishing closure on 
the effort and species biomass distribution over time, which can be quantified and visually 
displayed in the Ecospace output maps. We then adopted a scenario-based approach to test and 
compare the results of different fishing closure settings, where we implemented changes in the 
size, duration of the closure and the number of fleets involved (Table 5.3). Shrimp trawlers are 
not subject to the ban because they usually and only fish in the outer gulf (called zone 203, 
Figure 5.3) and artisanal manual collection is not part of the closure neither. 
We defined 6 scenarios to test the effects of different regulations of the fishing closure. The 
first scenario- traditional fishing closure- existed since 1990s. The inner and intermediate gulf 
if closed for all fleets, except for manual collection, for three months. In the next scenario (2), 
we test the effects of including manual collection to the closure, since its target species are 
intensively exploited. In the third scenario, we model the effect of a spatial extension of the 
fishing closure to the entire gulf. What would be the benefits for the target species to close the 
all zones) for fishing for 3 months? In the next scenario (4), we simulate the effects of a fishing 
closure that lasts for 4 months to ascertain the effects of a temporal extension. Scenario 5 tests 
how the recent changes in the regulations of the fishing closure effect the effort and catch 
distribution in the gulf. Recently, in response to the constraints of the COVID-19 pandemic and 
96 
the need for small pelagic fish as bait for commercial fisheries, INCOPESCA adjusted the 
regulations. Under the revised rules, the semi-industrial sardine fleet is allowed to fish 2 of 3 
closed months. 
The criteria to assess the effects of the different fishing closure setting (scenario 1-6) are 
measured as overall relative effort and catch by fleet, the relative biomass of the functional 
groups as well as spatial fishing effort distribution of the fleets and spatial biomass distribution 
of all functional groups. We also compare the long-term trends of catches for the fishing fleets 
(2013-2039).

5.2.3 Model Validation 
According to (Christensen and Walters 2024), the fishing effort distribution predicted by 
Ecospace should be compared to observed spatial effort patterns (VMS and/or AIS data), to 
validate and modify input parameters. In our study area, spatial data are rare, but we utilized 

97 
the limited available resources to validate our model performance. We used global fishing 
watch (GFW) data, based on data from the vessel monitoring system (VMS) provided by the 
Costa Rican Fisheries and Aquaculture Institute (INCOPESCA) to visually validate the model 
performance of fishing effort distribution. Furthermore, we used monitoring data from 
fishermen (2013) for a plausibility check of fleet and biomass distribution. 
Additionally, we conducted a sensitivity analysis to test the reliability of the Ecospace model. 
This included performing a robustness test following the guidelines outlined by  (Romagnoni 
et al. 2015, 2019). We tested different dispersal rate settings and evaluated the model sensitivity 
to alternative values using fictional MPAs in the model. The dispersal rate is a critical 
parameter, as the model performance is vulnerable to its setting. Accurate estimates are needed 
for model performance. Information on specific dispersal rates for species/functional group can 
be taken from ecological studies. However, such data are not always available, necessitating 
the use of multiple methods to estimate these parameters (Christensen and Walters 2024). A 
common assumption is to base the parameter on species lifestyle, coupled with sensitivity of 
the model to this parameter. 
 
5.3 Results 
5.3.1 An Ecosystem Without Shrimp Trawlers 
We compared a set of previously defined indicators, (see Section 2.2: Modelling background), 
between a period of intensive trawling (2013) to a projected time, 20 years after the last trawler 
left the gulf (2039). The Ecospace output results indicate that shrimp biomass of both shrimp 
groups (coastal and deep-sea) slowly increased after trawling until 2039 (Figure 5.4a). The 
increase of the coastal shrimp biomass is faster than the deep-sea shrimp biomass. The 
oscillation of the graph is due to the seasonal fishing closure

In the beginning of the modelling period (2013), the number of coastal shrimps landed by the 
artisanal fleet was similar to the number of landings by the semi-industrial trawlers. After the 
ban, the artisanal fleet slightly increased its catches; however, fishing mortality of coastal 
shrimps declined by around 40% (Figure 5.4b). In 2013, the deep-water shrimps were almost 
solely caught by the shrimp trawlers; thus, the fishing mortality declined drastically by 2039 
(Figure 5.4b). 
Besides the direct target species, the model indicates a recovery for several other species in the 
ecosystem. Bycatch species, such as Catfish, Morays & Eels and Small Demersals (Figure 5.5 
and Figure A.4 in the Appendix),), starts to recover. The biomass distribution of the predatory 
species, like Large Drums & Snook and Mackerel & Barracuda also increased in our model 
prediction by 2039. Rays & Shark distribution shifts more towards the inner Gulf of Nicoya, 
the same can be observed for Cephalopods (Figure 5.5 and Figure A.4 in the Appendix).

After the ban on trawling, the fishing effort distribution of the remaining fishing fleets also

effort can be also observed for the handline fleet. Effort is concentrated closer to the coast and 
ports, especially in the inner gulf (Figure 5.6).

The catches of all remaining fleets increased after 20 years of trawling ban (Table 5.4). The 
artisanal gillnet fleet, which targets similar species as the shrimp trawlers, increased its catches 
by 20%. The artisanal handline fleet could even increase catches by 70%. The longline and 
purse-seine fleet increased catches by around 10%. The increasing catches go along with 
marginal effort increases (by 1%). Thus, from the modelling result, all fleets increased the catch 
value. As the effort only increased slightly along with a minimum increase in costs, the overall 
profit of the fleets increased. The manual collection is an exception. It can be observed that the 
manual collection sector increased its fishing effort by 10% and over tripled the catch.

5.3.2 Optimization of the Seasonal Fishing Closure 
In the next step, we evaluated the efficiency of the seasonal fishing closure in the Gulf of 
Nicoya, under its original version, the revised regulation introduced by INCOPESCA and some 
alternative settings to potentially improve the success of the fishing closure (Scenario 1-6, Table 
5.5). Our modelling result revealed that the fishing closure can be an efficient measure to reduce 
the overall annual effort; however, its regulations that lead to the best performance remains 
unverified. Our modelling results indicate that the original fishing closure (Scenario 1) reduces 
the overall catch by 1.7% compared to a scenario without any restrictions (Scenario 6). The 
recent changes in regulation of the closure, allowing the sardines to fish 2 of the 3 months. This 
new fishing closure (Scenario 5) is less efficient with a lower annual catch reduction than the 
original fishing closure. A temporal extension of the seasonal closure from 3 to 4 months 
(Scenario 4) would lead to an extra reduction of 1.4% of the catch compared to the original 
fishing closure.

Till date, the manual collection is not tackled by the fishing closure, but our modelling results 
revealed that the biomasses of all target species of this fleet are below the initial biomass level 
of 2013. Figure 5.7 displays the biomass development of the target species until 2039 provided

the benefits of including the MC in the original closure and we see an addition 0.6% catch 
reduction over all.  
 
Figure 5.7 - Relative biomass of target species of the artisanal manual collection in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa

As the manual collection is also practiced along the shoreline of zone 203, we evaluated the 
effect of closing the entire gulf for all fleets for 3 months. The fishing closure reduces the annual 
fishing effort by an average of around 8% compared to allowing all fleets to fish throughout the 
year. Catch reduction, mostly from longline and sardine fleet, favors the recovery of small 
pelagics and large predatory species, not further straining the artisanal fleet. The scenario 
increases biomass distribution in the outer gulf of preadory species, catfish in coastal areas. 
Small pelagics and small demersal fish biomass increases in the outer gulf (Figure 5.8). 
A relative annual increase of the catches by all fleets running scenario 5 was later observed. 
Compared to 2013, our modeling results show an increase of the total catches by 2039. The 
annual catches increased for all fleets in 2039, the handline fleet increased its catches by 40% 
and the manual fleet even doubled its catches. This is the highest long-term increase in catches 
observed among all tested fishing closure settings (scenarios 1-6).

5.4 Discussion 
Our results suggest the need for further conservation measures in the Gulf of Nicoya. Shrimp 
trawlers have been identified as a major cause of ecosystem degradation; a pattern observed in 
many regions worldwide. The ecological consequences depend on the extent and intensity of 
trawling, and impact multiple components of the ecosystem. Firstly, trawlers can cause physical 
disturbance and even damage habitat structures (Kaiser et al. 2002; Turner et al. 1999). 
Moreover, as stated above, trawlers exert pressure on the demersal community structure through 
the extraction of substantial amounts of shrimps and enormous quantities of bycatch species. 
The biomass of bycatch species strongly exceeds the biomass of shrimps landed, leading to 
shrimp-to-bycatch ratios of 1:9 In the Gulf of Nicoya, the shrimp-to-bycatch ratio was estimated 
to be at least 1:7 and the species landed and discarded are diverse.  
A trawl ban provides a unique opportunity to investigate recoveries in ecosystem structure and 
function by removing the impacts from a major fishing gear operating in the ecosystem (Tao et 
al. 2021). The potential of a species to recover depends partly on its life history traits; smaller, 
short-lived species and fast-growing species, such as shrimps, tend to take a shorter time to 
recover from overfishing than larger species. However, we observed a slow recovery of shrimp 
biomass 20 years after the fishing closure, and the increase in relative biomass was not as 
significant as expected. This could be attributed to the status of the stock prior to the ban, which 
was strongly overexploited. Another reason for the slow recovery might be the influence of 

104 
predation mortality. As shown by our model, shrimps are the main prey for many species in the 
ecosystem. Thus, the reduction of fishing effort due to the ban on trawling and the associated 
increase in shrimp biomass availability, will lead to an increase in their predators, and thus in 
predation mortality. The predation mortality might be as high, that the shrimp stock biomass is 
not initially increasing. An additional factor, enhancing the predation effect, is that several 
predator species of shrimps have previously been caught as by-catch by the shrimp trawling 
fleet. After the ban the biomass of these groups increased, consequently the predation mortality 
on the shrimps further increased. As shown in other studies, middle and lower trophic levels 
may be sensitive to reductions in upper-level predator biomass (Hinke et al. 2004). The direct 
effects of reduced fishing mortality must be considered together with the indirect effects related 
to changes in predation and competition (Hinke et al. 2004). As in other studies, we can 
demonstrate that the speed of recovery significantly depends on the current status of the stock 
and it can vary among ecosystems and that recovery can be a challenging and prolonged 
process. For example, in the Indonesian shrimp fishery, the overfished shrimp stocks showed a 
recovery within a 7-year period after the ban (Chong et al. 1987). Another study in the Java Sea 
revealed a shift in shrimp species dominance after the ban, as well as an increase of shrimp 
biomass only in offshore waters, not in coastal areas (Tirtadanu and Suprato 2017). In Hong 
Kong waters, the shrimp biomass remained unchanged for the next 5 years after the ban (Mak 
et al. 2021). We observed a faster biomass increase of coastal than deep-sea shrimps in our 
model. This can be due to a more intense targeting of shrimp predators in coastal waters due to 
the operational radius of the artisanal fleets, compared to deeper waters, where the shrimp 
trawlers used to operate. Besides the effect of predation mortality, this can also be due to their 
lower initial biomass of deep-sea shrimps or lower production/biomass ratio.  
Beside the recovery of shrimps as main target species, we expected the ban on trawling to have 
a positive effect on bycatch species, ocean-bottom flora and fauna as well as demersal bottom-
associated species. In the Gulf of Nicoya, the bycatch species, such as catfish, morays and eels, 
and small demersals showed marked recovery initially. As shown in an embayment in Kenya 
(Munga et al. 2012), and in (estuarine) coastal waters of Hong Kong, China (Mak et al. 2021) 
and Indonesia (Bailey 1997) and the Mediterranean (Pipitone et al. 2023), the recovery of the 
demersal fish and shellfish assemblages, can be faster than the increase of shrimp biomass. The 
increasing abundance of lower trophic level species will most likely be followed by an increase 
in biomass of longer-lived predatory species, like drums and snook, mackerel, barracuda and 
sharks. Among these, demersal sharks and rays were particularly vulnerable bycatch species in 
the trawl fisheries of the Gulf of Nicoya (Clarke et al. 2016) that could benefit from the ban. 
The biomass distribution of predatory species, such as large mackerel & barracuda also 
increased in our model prediction by 2039. Rays and shark biomass increased slightly and the 
distribution of this group shifted more towards the inner Gulf of Nicoya, and the same can be 
observed for cephalopods. The predatory species follow the distribution of the prey species, 
such as small demersals, snappers and catfish. Some species do not exhibit an upward trend, 
which can be due to an increase in predation mortality caused by the increase in predator 
biomass. Notably, potential habitat destruction that might have been caused by the process of 
bottom trawling and its recovery after the ban is not part of the Ecospace model.  Besides the 
changes in biomass distribution, the removal of the trawling effort also caused local shifts in 
the fishing efforts of the remaining fleets. These shifts can be related to the distribution of target 
105 
species. For instance, the handline fleet shifts its effort to the inner gulf, concentrating the effort 
on zone 201, following the distribution of its main target species, such as small demersal, 
snappers and catfish. The same process can be observed for the manual fleet. The semi-
industrial sardine fleet is slightly affected by the change in fleet effort. This was expected, as 
these two fleets did not overlap in their target/landed species. The sardine fleet operates mainly 
in zone 203, and targets specifically small pelagic fish with a low bycatch ratio. 
Overall, we see clear benefits of the ban on trawling for the ecosystem. However, the ecosystem 
has been strongly degraded over the last 30 years and this conservational measure might not be 
sufficient to achieve a recovery of the majority of functional groups across the ecosystem. We 
know from other studies, that a simultaneous recovery of all species in the system is unlikely 
to be achieved due to various trophic interactions and other complex external influences (Froese 
et al. 2016; Steven Mackinson et al. 2009)  but we believe that for the Gulf of Nicoya more 
measures are necessary to turn the tide. Thus, we assessed fishing closures as a further 
conservational measure in the Gulf of Nicoya. The three-month closed fishing season in the 
inner and intermediate Gulf of Nicoya, known as ‘veda’ in Spanish has been applied for many 
years now. Seasonal closures have often been implemented to protect over-exploited spawning 
stocks in certain locations, but the effectiveness of these measures is rarely tested (Clarke et al., 
2015). However, these measures, for example, in the case of Atlantic cod off the coast of 
Scotland, are easier to implement and control for single stocks. (Clarke et al., 2015). In multi-
species fisheries, adjusting the closure to meet the needs of many different target species is 
challenging (Samy-Kamal et al., 2015).  
The performance of the temporary marine protected area was compared with different levels of 
protection. The traditional seasonal fishing closure reduces the total annual catch by 2.2% 
compared to a situation without any restrictions. In recent years, due to the increasing economic 
challenges exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic, the regulations governing the seasonal 
closure have changed. The sardine purse seiners are partially allowed to fish during the closed 
season. Nowadays, sardine vessels are banned for only one of the three months, with the 
justification that sardines are sold as bait for the national fishing fleet. The total annual catch 
reduction of the closure with the new regulations is 1.8%, lower than during the traditional 
closure. On the one hand, our model does not indicate a significant negative effect of the small 
pelagic purse-seiners operating during the fishing closure, as the fleet operates selectively, 
exclusively catching its target species, and its main fishing ground is the outer Gulf, which is 
not part of the fishing closure. On the other hand, the exclusive right to fish during the seasonal 
closure could cause an unfavorable shift of effort into the Gulf. Furthermore, favoring the semi-
industrial fleet against the artisanal fleet is not recommendable, as it can foster competition and 
decrease the compliance of the artisanal fishing communities. According to our modelling 
results, a temporal extension of the fishing closure by one month is of little benefit to the 
ecosystem and would cause a loss of income/ for the artisanal fleets.  
From a conservation standpoint, our model results suggest that manual collection activities 
should be considered in the seasonal closure. Due to the downward trend of all its target species, 
we advise against allowing manual collection to operate during the closed season, as it operates 
above a sustainable threshold, especially concerning the bivalve extraction. Regarding 
declining benthic biomass, the impact of the artisanal fleet should not be disregarded. The heavy 
106 
exploitation of bivalves by coastal communities (yearly average = 20 metric tons in the last 5 
years) has most likely caused the severe decline in epibenthos biomass. These species declined 
together with the loss of mangrove areas.  A positive development was seen in other parts of 
Costa Rica. In the neighboring estuary, Golfo Dulce in Costa Rica INCOPESCA saw the urge 
to act and established a special fishing closure that permits the extraction of the bivalve species 
Anadara tuberculosa and Anadara similis in certain areas of the mangrove forest for the entire 
year 2023.  
A fishing closure that includes all fleets increases efficiency but we see from our modelling 
results that the effect of the closure on the catches and biomass distribution of species could be 
enhanced. Thus, we tested a fishing closure covering the entire Gulf and including all fleets. 
Our results indicate that this fishing closure will lead to an annual catch reduction of 
approximately 10%. This is due to a substantial decrease of fishing effort, that benefits all 
species in the Gulf. We see increasing biomasses across almost all functional groups. Our 
biomass distribution maps indicate increasing biomasses of high trophic levels in the outer Gulf. 
Given that the fishing closure is only temporary, fishing effort remains high during the rest of 
the year, and the modelling results indicate benefits for almost all species, we recommend 
implementing a complete closure of the Gulf to all fleets. These closure settings, without 
exceptions, also simplify the enforcement and control of regulations. Regarding the benefits of 
the fishing closure, we should consider that the closed season is intended to support the hatching 
and reproduction of several target species in the Gulf of Nicoya. Our model does not assess the 
success of these endeavors and does not fully account for these additional closure benefits. 
Besides the environmental benefits of these conservational measures, we also need to see the 
socioeconomic importance of the fisheries for many people. For example, manual collection 
significantly impacts the coastal communities, particularly for women engaged in this 
occupation. This is also applicable to sardine fisheries, as many people depend on the fishing 
sector. The seasonal fishing closure has an impact on the income and livelihood of the fishermen 
and leads to short-term disadvantages, especially for the fishermen who already live on the 
margins of subsistence (Brillo et al. 2019; Islam et al. 2021). Ideally, subsidies and alternative 
employment opportunities would be needed during fishing closure periods but in reality, there 
are often no alternative employment opportunities, subsidies are insufficient and alternative 
sources of income are rare (Colwell and Axelrod 2017; Owusu and Andriesse 2020) . In some 
areas, artisanal fishermen who collect bivalves have been alternatively employed for the 
restoration of mangroves, providing a livelihood as well as ecosystem services to fish, shrimp 
and society. One also needs to consider the long-term benefits of the fishing closure. Catches 
can be higher after the closure and potentially reduce the travel distances from the port, leading 
to lower operating costs (Samy-Kamal et al., 2015). From an efficient fishing closure, we would 
expect increasing biomasses of the target stocks over the years, which would lead to higher 
catches of the fishing fleets.  However, the short-term disadvantages often outweigh the 
benefits. 
A permanent Marine Protected Areas (MPAs) could be an efficient measure of conservation in 
the Gulf of Nicoya, which has not yet been implemented. MPAs are a tool to safeguard marine 
natural systems, yet their effectiveness depends on how well they are integrated into the existing 
socioeconomic context (Horta e Costa et al. 2022). The problem of implementing a permanent 
107 
MPA in the Gulf of Nicoya is the socioeconomic structure of the fishing sector. The artisanal 
fishing fleet fishes from small ports close to their communities and the manual collection has 
an even smaller operational radius. Closing one certain area permanently puts high constraints 
on certain communities close to the area, cutting them out from their fishing grounds. The high 
level of subsistence fishing in the Gulf of Nicoya would make it even more difficult to enforce 
and control an MPA in certain areas. A permanent MPA in deeper waters further away from 
the coast would be more feasible from a socioeconomic point of view but mostly likely not the 
most efficient from a conservational point of view. The advanced artisanal fleet and the semi-
industrial fleet cover larger distances from the port with advanced boats and gears and are 
impacted less severely by a stationary permanent MPA. Further ecological research to identify 
the definition of optimal trade-offs between alternative potential areas would be needed together 
with a socioeconomic evaluation of the negative impacts on the artisanal fishing communities.  
Ecospace is a spatial modelling tool that allows for considering the impact of MPAs in an 
ecosystem context. It models the distribution of species biomass due to changes in effort 
distribution of the different fishing fleets and other possible factors. Ecospace has the ability to 
account for spatial effects in the ecosystem that are of importance for assessing the potential 
benefits of MPAs, such as the concentration of fishing effort at the edge of the MPAs and high 
movement rates of species. Cascade effects may generate a high concentration of prey outside 
the MPA, and attract predators out of the protected areas (Walters et al. 1999). Furthermore, 
these effects cannot be captured by non-spatial models. Ecospace can also be used to find policy 
alternatives that can afterwards be validated by more detailed analysis and experimental field 
testing. In this study, we utilize some of the capabilities of the Ecospace model to study the 
ecological effects of alternative MPAs settings, which could not be done with a non-spatial 
model. Ecospace was able to capture the effects of the interaction between spatial effort and 
species distribution and the predator-prey dynamics inside and outside the closure. These 
elements help to guide informed decisions which are based on ecosystem considerations. 
 
5.5 Conclusion 
We conclude that banning all shrimp trawlers form the Gulf of Nicoya is an important step 
towards conservation. This action is expected to benefit many species, improve the condition 
of bottom habitats and support the entire health of the ecosystem. However, with artisanal fleet 
likely to increase its catch in the future due to the loss of competition from the shrimp trawlers, 
some additional restrictions might be necessary, as the fishing closure with its recent settings 
might be insufficient. Our modelling results indicate that a closed season comprising the entire 
gulf including all fleets could be a promising measure, limiting also the extraction of mollusks 
and crustaceans from the gulf. A permanent MPA in the Gulf of Nicoya could be a good 
measure for conservation. In the next step, these questions can be assessed using the Ecospace 
model. The model’s spatial optimization tool for protected area placement, which is an 
automated tool, can be used to find the appropriate areas for the placement of MPAs. The tool 
automatically detects suitable areas (cells) for protection by maximizing ecological, social and 
economic criteria (Christensen et al. 2009). This approach aligns with the policy search routine 
applied by (Alms, Romagnoni, and Wolff 2022). 
108 
 
  
GENERAL DISCUSSION 
  
109 
6.1 Main Findings 
We used the full scope of the EwE software package to assess the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem, 
its past and present state, and its potential future development. To some extent, what we found 
is alarming. Thus, we also presented some possible future management scenarios to respond to 
the negative trend.  
Our general analysis of the characteristics of the Gulf of Nicoya presented a productive bottom-
up controlled system with few species at the intermediate level. The importance of 
phytoplankton and zooplankton is highly relative, driving the extremely productive system. At 
the same time, we highlight the importance of links to higher trophic levels (benthos, shrimps, 
and small pelagics), all species heavily targeted by the fisheries.  These results are consistent 
with findings in other coastal or semi-enclosed ecosystems. Primary producers, zooplankton, 
small pelagics, and benthic organisms have been frequently observed to be among the most 
vital groups, e.g., in Chesapeake Bay (Baird and Ulanowicz 1989), the Gulf of Maine (Heymans 
2001), and the Gulf of Thailand (Christensen 1998). Small pelagic fish, also called “wasp-
waist” species, have been shown to exert top-down control on their prey species and bottom-up 
control on their predators (Shannon et al. 2007). 
Our main finding concerning the fisheries’ impact on the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem was a 
steady overexploitation since 1995 and a continuous degradation of the system, affecting the 
trophic level structure of the whole ecosystem and reducing the biomasses of many high trophic 
level species. These unfavorable developments are accompanied by severe economic losses for 
the semi-industrial and artisanal fleet, threatening subsistence fishing. In our attempt to analyze 
the reasons for the downward trend of the ecosystem, we found overfishing to be the main, but 
not the sole, reason/driver. Besides the fishing pressure, climate variations could be identified 
as an additional driver for ecosystem change. Our time-series models thus showed that the 
variations in the fisheries catch could not solely be explained by fishing pressure variations as 
shown in other systems (Shannon et al., 2008).  In our study, the Chlorophyll a concentration 
appeared to be the most relevant of tested environmental drivers for influencing the stocks of 
several functional groups (large drums and snook, crabs and morays, and eels).  The 
environmental changes in the Gulf of Nicoya are influenced by the large-scale ecological 
conditions associated with the ENSO cycle.  
We found some important potential countermeasures in our attempt to propose a way out of the 
negative trend. The ban on trawling, or at least a severe reduction of trawling, is an essential 
measure to allow for shrimp population recovery. Shrimp recovery is crucial for the entire 
ecosystem as shrimps are a fundamental link between trophic levels in the system. A trawling 
ban would also allow bycatch species regeneration, favorably impacting the ecosystem. 
However, a trawling ban alone is not sufficient for the full recovery of the ecosystem. These 
findings and the social and economic consequences of eliminating shrimp trawling need to be 
considered.  
We also learned from our study that participatory management and the engagement of 
stakeholders in the process of policymaking can be successful and go well, along with the use 
of the Ecopath with Ecosim software. 
110 
6.1.1 Ecosystem Shifts 
Compared to the status of the ecosystem in the early 90s (baseline model, (Mathias Wolff et al. 
1998)) we detected a further degradation of the overall ecosystem health. (1) We see a decline 
in mangrove coverage by 10% due to land use changes. (2) A decrease in coastal shrimp species 
caused a shift in fishing efforts towards capturing deep-water shrimp species in the outer gulf. 
(3) A decline in bycatch species such as eel and catfish (4) and an overall decrease in the 
biomass of benthic invertebrates due to shrimp trawling, the excessive manual collection of 
bivalves, and the loss of the mangrove area. (5) A parallel decline in small pelagic fish biomass 
could be observed due to the immense fishing pressure of the semi-industrial fleet, leaving a 
hole in the intermediate trophic level, a vital link between the productive lower levels and the 
higher trophic levels, causing trophic cascades throughout the food web. (6) Thus, biomasses 
and catches of long-lived, high-trophic-level fish (rays and sharks) decreased. Our analysis 
highlights the importance of the mid-trophic level species, which are heavily exploited by semi-
industrial fisheries (small pelagics) and artisanal fleets (small demersal). The observed decline 
of these species did not only have ecological, economic, and social consequences (Alms and 
Wolff 2019; Queiros et al. 2019) but may also lead to unfavorable shifts in the ecosystem. 
6.1.2 Economic Loss 
Although fishing efforts and overall catches increased between 1993 and 2013, these catches’ 
total value and profit decreased substantially over the two decades of interest, causing a 
tremendous economic loss of approximately 50%. This loss of profits was due to changes in 
the catches toward low-value species, such as small pelagic, snappers, and small demersal 
species. In 2013, shrimps accounted for over 28% of the catch’s total value and constituted only 
half as much as that in 1993.  
These developments caused an expanding fishery for low-trophic-level species with low 
economic value while valuable shrimp resources and higher-trophic-level species were 
depleted. Both trends - fishing more heavily on low-trophic-level species and the expansion of 
the spatial scale to areas where large fish are still more abundant - are common phenomena in 
tropical fisheries (Pauly et al. 1998), along with advancements in the gears and modernization 
of the semi-industrial fleet (Vega-Corrales and Marín 2014). In the early 2000s, several illegal 
gillnets with smaller mesh sizes were introduced into the Gulf of Nicoya, potentially increasing 
the yield of smaller and juvenile fish and increasing the success of catching small, low-value 
coastal fish. 
6.1.3 Drivers of Ecosystem Change 
Our results indicate that the fisheries’ exploitation rates mainly drive variations in the catch of 
some target species (e.g., cephalopods and shrimps). This is an expected result, especially for 
shrimps which are heavily fished. However, climate variations also substantially affect other 
species (e.g., corvina, snook, small pelagics, and crabs). The Chlorophyll-a  concentration 
appeared to be the most relevant among tested environmental drivers. It is a biomass proxy for 
phytoplankton in the water column, which directly affects the bottom of the food chain. 
Increasing phytoplankton availability enhances zooplankton growth, resulting in higher prey 
availability for low trophic level species foraging on zooplankton. The associated increased 
production will then cascade through the entire food web. 
111 
Furthermore, the main finding of our study is that the Gulf of Nicoya is influenced by large-
scale environmental conditions associated with the ENSO cycle. We observed variations in the 
catches of some trophic groups (large drums and snook, crabs, morays, and eels) related to the 
ENSO cycle. El Niño periods are associated with high Sea Surface Temperature (SST) extremes 
and increased precipitation. During these periods, phytoplankton productivity and zooplankton 
biomass decrease with bottom–up effects on the entire food chain.   
We propose considering the system’s environmental indicators and fishing effort regulations to 
form and develop appropriate management strategies. Thus, a stringent effort control that 
incorporates a buffer for external effects will help the ecosystem recover and increase its 
resilience towards climate change in the future. 
 
6.2 Recommended Policy Plans/Management Implications 
The results of this study confirm the urgency for the enforcement of regulations to protect the 
marine coastal resources in the Gulf of Nicoya. From our modelling results, we can state that a 
severe reduction of shrimp trawling is essential for recovering the Gulf of Nicoya ecosystem. 
All tested management scenarios that include the goals of increasing ecosystem health and 
biodiversity suggest a reduction of trawling by 50–100%. Despite the benefits of the ban on 
trawling, we see from our modelling results that it is insufficient to fully recover biomass levels 
of overall functional groups, especially of the key species in the system. Thus, two possible 
alternative management scenarios are proposed. Both call for additional reductions in 
fishing efforts, mainly by the semi-industrial purse seine fleet and the artisanal longline fleet. 
We also present the measures necessary to restore functional group biomass levels to the known 
state in 1993 (baseline model by Alms and Wolff 2019; Wolff et al. 1998), disregarding the 
feasibility and socio-economic aspects. In this scenario, the extent of ecosystem degradation is 
highlighted. Necessary measures would involve a ban on trawling and a minimum reduction of 
gillnet, handline, and purse seine fishing. The scenario would only allow the longline fleet to 
operate in the Gulf of Nicoya, together with manual collection, leading to an 80% loss of profit 
and social value. 
Before implementing new policy measures, it can be beneficial to strengthen the enforcement 
and control mechanisms of already-existing regulations. One example of this would be the 
protection plan in the frame of the “Marine Area of Multiple Use” (Area Marina de Uso 
Multiple [AMUM]) implemented in the Gulf of Nicoya by the Costa Rican government in 1995 
to protect and conserve marine and coastal resources through integrated management. To 
protect the marine resources in the AMUM, a “Veda” (an annual 3-month period) is 
implemented in which the Gulf of Nicoya is closed to fisheries. The temporary closure is 
supposed to overlap with the reproduction peak of commercially important species to support 
their recovery and aims to reduce fishing efforts in general. We suggest keeping and potentially 
extending the closure of seasonal spatial fisheries in the Gulf of Nicoya.  
One could also tackle the severe problem of illegal mesh sizes in the Gulf of Nicoya. A 
minimum mesh size of 7.62 cm (3 in) for gillnets was implemented with little success. 
However, studies show that small meshed gillnets are not necessarily more destructive, as they 
target a smaller size range of fish,  and may instead promote sustained production by allowing 
112 
a higher proportion of the spawning biomass to remain in the stock (Wolff et al. 2015). 
However, the specimens are removed from the gulf at a small size and generate low market 
values for fishermen. Our assessment results highlight the discrepancy between socioeconomic 
and conservational goals and the challenge of finding the right balance for policy-making. 
Increasing profits comes with improving the semi-industrial fishing effort at the expense of the 
ecosystem health. Efforts reduction of the artisanal fleet in favor of ecosystem health entail the 
loss of jobs for the fishermen in the communities around the Gulf of Nicoya. Ideally, policies 
that facilitate alternative income opportunities can be provided, some of which have already 
been implemented in other coastal regions. A good example of an alternative occupation in 
Costa Rica is the mangrove restoration. Artisanal fishermen who used to collect bivalves from 
the mangrove sediments have been alternatively employed to restore mangroves, planting 
mangrove trees in coastal areas. This occupation provides a livelihood for the fishermen and 
ecosystem services for fish, shrimp, and society. The same actions have been taken in other 
marine areas like India, Malaysia, Nigeria, and Panama (BDCP 2007; Porter and Orams 2014; 
Suman 2019; Woods et al., 2022). However, first of all, the burden of financing the Mangrove 
replanting program has to be overcome. These projects have been carried out as community 
initiatives, government-sponsored projects, or large-scale international development assistance 
programs (Primavera and Esteban 2008).  
This study exemplifies how holistic ecosystem models can be used for management advice 
and how stakeholders can be engaged in policymaking.  In eight participatory management 
workshops developed by our partners (Sánchez- Jiménez et al. 2019; Sánchez Jiménez et al. 
2021), stakeholders have been invited, and ecological information about the Gulf of Nicoya 
ecosystem have been presented using our EwE models. It is a promising approach of 
participatory management in the Gulf of Nicoya to listen to the advice/perception of the 
fishermen themselves in stakeholder workshops and interviews. The EwE food web flowcharts 
are a descriptive illustration of the ecosystem that can serve as a basis for discussion. The 
suggested effort reduction by the fishermen was thereafter used for our policy search. We 
believe cooperation with stakeholders is crucial for designing future management plans and 
benefitting all parties involved. Management authorities can learn from the fishermen’s local 
ecological knowledge, and science has the right tools to develop reliable fisheries management 
plans that minimize these livelihood-threatening uncertainties. We suggest that stakeholder 
engagement can be particularly intensified in the entire EwE modelling processes, starting with 
data collection (here, the discussion with fishermen on relevant biological groups to be 
considered for the model construction may be a very relevant first step), continuing with model 
development and model validation, and ending with management simulations. As shown in 
several cases, stakeholder engagement can be used for management strategy evaluation (Fulton 
et al. 2011; Koenigstein et al. 2016) as a stakeholder-informed ecosystem modelling approach 
and is a key aspect in providing good governance, dissolving top-down hierarchies and 
improving compliance of all actors (Pita et al. 2010; Soma 2003). 
 
 
 
113 
6.3 Critical Assessment of the Methodological Approach  
Multi-species models per se do not always provide better advice than single-species models, 
which are still necessary for the management of many fisheries (Hollowed et al. 2000). 
However, we believe that depending on the research questions and data availability, the Ecopath 
with Ecosim software offers excellent tools for a detailed analysis of the ecosystem 
(Christensen and Steenbeek 2024). We successfully integrated available data on trophic 
interactions and external drivers in the model and were able to identify and separate the role of 
fishery and the environment in driving ecosystemic changes over time. Thus, multi-species 
models can potentially offer a greater biological reality concerning the incorporation of trophic 
interactions and the role of environmental disturbance (Hollowed et al. 2000).   
The integrated policy search routine implemented in Ecosim is a valuable tool for identifying 
optimum levels of fishing efforts for multiple fleets that may each exploit numerous species 
from an ecosystem. Moreover, this method allows exploring the effects of different 
management policies on the ecosystem and the socio-economic well-being of the resource 
users. The spatial modelling tool Ecospace is one choice among others to visualize the 
efficiency of the seasonal fishing closure, but one of few that allows monitoring ecosystem and 
fisheries components at once.  
This dissertation’s different analyses are based on one trophic reference model. The quality and 
robustness of that model are crucial for every step in the assessment. Therefore, the 
correctness/quality of the input data is fundamental for the accuracy of the assessment of the 
results. Thus, all input data need to be examined and data quality validated. Data inserted had 
to be checked manually for plausibility and were also compared to other models and literature 
(e.g., diet composition). The Ecopath software uses a pedigree system to calculate the overall 
data quality fed into the model to support data validation. The calculated EwE Pedigree Index 
of 0.29, based on the confidence intervals of all input parameters, indicated that the model’s 
level of precision is in the intermediate to the upper range compared to other published Ecopath 
models worldwide (Morissette 2007). The Ecopath models used in our study were also carefully 
validated with the prescribed balancing routine of Link (2010), as described in Chapter 2. We 
adhered to the suggestions of best practices in EwE food web models for ecosystem-based 
management by Heymans et al. (2016).  
In the next step, it is mandatory for good scientific practice to fit the model to time-series data 
to observe whether the model can predict observed/measured values in a correct manner. This 
step has been carefully fulfilled for our model, which was calibrated with historical fishing data 
sets, and comparing the model predictions to reference data allowed for the validation of the 
model performance (Heymans et al. 2016). We followed the recommendation to adapt the 
Akaike Information Criterion (AIC) for small sample sizes and used the bias-corrected version 
AICc instead (Burnham et al. 2011). We believe that the AICc is a good measure for validating 
the selected model.  
We also carefully tried to validate our Ecospace model. According to  Christensen and Walters 
(2024), the fishing effort distribution predicted by the model should be compared to observed 
spatial effort patterns (vessel monitoring system and/or AIS data) to validate and modify input 
parameters. Spatial data are rare in our study area, but we used limited resources to validate our 
114 
model performance. We used global fishing watch (GFW) data based on data from the vessel 
monitoring system (VMS) provided by INCOPESCA to visually validate the model 
performance. In addition, we monitored data from fishermen (2013) for a plausibility check of 
fleet and species distribution. We took these steps to validate our model and ensure the best 
possible performance. However, we should remember that a model is no more than a good 
estimation of reality.  
A critical point of our study was general data availability. We were working in a data-deficient 
region, and the data resolution was not comparable to the data-rich regions (Northeast US) or 
data-rich stocks (Bigeye Tuna) in some other areas of the world (Wiedenmann et al. 2019; Li 
et al. 2022). Unfortunately, we have been unable to procure fisheries data on effort and landings 
in recent years. While we reported a substantial reduction of the officially reported fishing effort 
in the gulf, it can be assumed that unreported catches, fishing without licenses, and illegal 
fishing during the closed season have occurred during the observational period. These 
unreported fishing activities could not be quantified, a problem common in many fishing 
regions worldwide. The availability of recent fisheries d ata on the Gulf of Nicoya is scarce; 
thus, simulations with data from the last few years were impossible. The good news is that there 
is progress. Nowadays, Costa Rica is introducing GPS tracking to some of the vessels to act 
against illegal fishing. Another great initiative is the fact that Costa Rican Fisheries and 
Aquaculture Institute has collaborated with Global Fishing Watch, an initiative that uses 
satellite imagery, vessel GPS data, and machine learning to monitor fishing activity at sea to 
ensure a fair and sustainable use of the ocean (https://globalfishingwatch.org/).  
Our study gives insights into the past and present status of the Gulf of Nicoya and the drivers 
of the observed changes that occurred over time. We assessed the possibilities of combining the 
commercial interest of artisanal and commercial fisheries with the conservation of the 
ecosystem and the biomass recovery of different functional groups. We also simulated and 
visualized in a spatial model the distribution of species biomasses and fishing efforts related to 
the ban on trawling and marine protected areas. Our study contributes to the Ecosystem 
Approach to Fisheries and presents numerous analyses possible with the Ecopath with Ecosim 
software. We also provide an example of an interdisciplinary work that aims to take the first 
step in fostering participatory management in the Gulf of Nicoya. 
  
115

Hiermit erkläre ich, dass ich die Doktorarbeit mit dem Titel „ Modelling Two Decades of 
Change and Future Developments in the Gulf of Nicoya (Costa Rica) Ecosystem Using the 
Ecopath with Ecosim Toolbox “ selbstständig verfasst und geschrieben habe und außer den 
angegebenen Quellen keine weiteren Hilfsmittel verwendet habe. 
Ebenfalls erkläre ich hiermit, dass es sich bei den mir abgegebenen Arbeiten um drei identische 
Exemplare handelt.
